Multidimensional Stability of Planar Travelling Waves for Stochastically Perturbed Reaction-Diffusion Systems
M. van den Bosch, H. J. Hupkes

Abstract
We consider reaction-diffusion systems with multiplicative noise on a spatial domain of dimension two or higher. The noise process is white in time, coloured in space, and invariant under translations. Inspired by previous works on the real line, we establish the multidimensional stability of planar waves on a cylindrical domain on time scales that are exponentially long with respect to the noise strength. This is achieved by means of a stochastic phase tracking mechanism that can be maintained over such long time scales. The corresponding mild formulation of our problem features stochastic integrals with respect to anticipating integrands, which hence cannot be understood within the well-established setting of Itô-integrals. To circumvent this problem, we exploit and extend recently developed theory concerning forward integrals.

Keywords:
propagating fronts and pulses, translation invariant noise, nonlinear stability, stochastic phase shift, forward integrals

1
Introduction

In this paper we set out to study the multidimensional stability of planar travelling wave solutions to stochastic reaction-diffusion systems of the form

\mathrm{d}u=[D\Delta u+f(u)]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma g(u)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q},

(1.1)

posed on the cylindrical spatial domain

\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
for some
d\geq 2
, where
\mathbb{T}
denotes a
one-dimensional torus of arbitrary fixed size. The waves of interest will be propagating in the direction of the first (unbounded) coordinate. In addition,
for
\textbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}
and
t>0
we have
u(\mathbf{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\geq 1
,
and the matrix
D
is diagonal with strictly positive entries. The deterministic dynamics is driven by a “generalised Gaussian” noise process which is considered white in time and coloured in space. The noise satisfies the formal relations

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q}(\textbf{x})]

\displaystyle=0,

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q}(\textbf{x})\mathrm{d}W_{t^{\prime}}^{Q}(\textbf{x}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=\delta(t-t^{\prime})q(\textbf{x}-\textbf{x}^{\prime}),

for some smooth covariance function
q
that characterises the correlation in space. In particular,
the noise and hence system (
1.1
) are translationally invariant. The
Q
refers to the convolution operator induced by
q.
As currently written, the noise in (
1.1
) should be interpreted in the Itô sense, but for many physical applications—especially involving external noise—it is more natural to interpret stochastic terms in the Stratonovich sense
[
44
,
95
]
. We denote such stochastic reaction-diffusion systems as

\mathrm{d}u=[D\Delta u+f(u)]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma g(u)\circ\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q},

(1.3)

and note that these can also be incorporated into our framework. This paper is based on preceding works where
d=1
; see
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
]
. We emphasise that in
[
30
]
the mathematical expressions (
1.1
), (
1.2
), and (
1.3
) are linked back to the relevant equations and notation commonly used in the physics literature.

Our aim is to explore the influence of the multiplicative noise term for small
\sigma
on the propagation of planar wave solutions. As is discussed in
[
19
]
and several references therein, multiplicative noise is associated with external fluctuations. Typical scenarios are whenever a control parameter of the otherwise deterministic system fluctuates around its intended value. For example, in chemical systems—especially within experimental setups—the reaction rates serve as control parameters that are highly sensitive to local variations in temperature, illumination conditions in photochemical systems and other environmental variables
[
36
,
66
,
89
]
.
As an illustration, let us consider the effect of fluctuations in the intensity of the light source driving photosensitive Belousov-Zhabotinskii chemical reactions. The authors in
[
84
]
demonstrate, both numerically and experimentally, that for an effectively one-dimensional front the velocity decreases when random spatial fluctuations of the light intensity are present. In the two-dimensional setting, however, the front becomes
distorted and curvature seems to favour an increase in the wave velocity.

One may mathematically model the latter by stochastically perturbing, in an appropriate manner, the two-component system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u

\displaystyle=\Delta u+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(u-u^{2}-(\alpha v+\beta)\frac{u-\gamma}{u+\gamma}\right),

(1.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v

\displaystyle=\delta\Delta v+u-v,

where
u
and
v
are dimensionless versions of the concentrations of bromous acid and the catalyst, respectively, while the parameters satisfy

\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\varepsilon>0
. These equations are derived in
[
46
]
from a modified version of the Oregenator model by performing a quasi-steady state approximation. These (minor) modifications are motivated by the fact that inhibiting effects were observed after irradiating photosensitive chemicals
with both ultraviolet and visible light
[
97
]
.

The control parameter
\beta
in system (
1.4
) is proportional to the applied light intensity. Note that existence and temporal stability of travelling waves, and other spatial patterns such as spirals, have been extensively studied
in the light-insensitive case (i.e.,
\beta=0
); see
[
23
,
43
,
54
,
64
]
and references therein.
Allowing

\beta
to fluctuate randomly by performing the formal substitution
\beta\mapsto\beta-\varepsilon\sigma\frac{\partial}{\partial t}W_{t}^{Q}
,
we obtain the stochastically perturbed system

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\left[\Delta u+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(u-u^{2}-(\alpha v+\beta)\frac{u-\gamma}{u+\gamma}\right)\chi(u)\right]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\frac{u-\gamma}{u+\gamma}\pi(u)\circ\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q},

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\left[\delta\Delta v+u-v\right]\mathrm{d}t,

after
introducing cut-off functions
\chi
and
\pi
that satisfy

\chi(u)=\pi(u)=1
for all values of
u
that are chemically relevant.
We exploit such cut-off functions to enforce convenient
pointwise
Lipschitz properties on certain nonlinearities and to ensure
that the noise does not affect the homogeneous background states
of the front solutions.
We refer to
[
27
,
30
]
for many other examples of model systems that fit within our framework.

Deterministic setting

From now on, we shall consider the setting where (
1.1
) with
\sigma=0
admits a spectrally stable travelling front or pulse in one spatial dimension. More specifically, the operator associated to the linearisation about the wave in the one-dimensional setting is assumed to have only the translational eigenvalue at zero, with the remainder of the spectrum bounded away from the imaginary axis in the left half plane.
Such spectrally stable waves are known to exist under quite general hypotheses
[
20
,
27
,
40
,
82
]
. A common analytical approach for establishing this spectral gap condition
is to invoke methods from geometric singular perturbation theory
[
26
,
31
]
, which rely on a strict separation between the diffusive length scales. In terms of the example system (
1.4
) this would mean
\delta\ll 1
, which for instance is a natural assumption when modelling the propagation of waves in a silica gel where the catalyst is being immobilised
[
46
,
84
]
.

The spectral gap allowed Kapitula
[
38
,
39
]

to use semigroup methods to prove under very mild conditions that the associated planar waves on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are orbitally stable for every
d\geq 2
. Our main purpose here is to use the spirit of his approach to establish similar conclusions for stochastically forced systems.
Earlier deterministic approaches, such as
[
56
]
, depend heavily on the maximum principle and energy methods which are generally not applicable to most systems, which we therefore also choose to avoid. It is also worth pointing out that the spectrum of the operator associated to the linearisation of a planar wave
(d\geq 2)
is no longer bounded away from the imaginary axis. In particular, the resulting algebraic decay of perturbations required the use of a refined decomposition in which the phase of the wave plays a crucial role.

Cylindrical spatial domain

The first main reason to restrict to a cylindrical spatial domain is our desire to consider noise that is translationally invariant.
In more detail, suppose
(W_{t}^{Q})_{t\geq 0}
is a cylindrical
Q
-Wiener process in
1
1

1

In fact, the noise process is rigorously constructed in an extended space
\mathcal{W}_{\rm ext}\supset\mathcal{W}
, see §
3.2
and
[
30
]
.
some space
\mathcal{W}
, where
Q:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W}
is a linear, symmetric, positive semi-definite operator
Q:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W}
. All these assumptions on
Q
are basically essential to make sense of it as a covariance operator. For us to be able to interpret either system (
1.1
) or (
1.3
),
it is important that stochastic integrals of the form

\int_{0}^{t}g(\Phi_{0})\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q}

(1.6)

are well-defined in a sense suitable for our analysis.
More specifically, we need to be able to interpret
g(\Phi_{0})
as a Hilbert-Schmidt operator from
\mathcal{W}_{Q}=Q^{1/2}(\mathcal{W})
to the Sobolev space
H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for some
k\geq 0
.
Consider now
\mathcal{W}=L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})
to be the space of square-integrable functions, where
m>0
denotes the number of noise components; we have
n=2
and
m=1
in system (
1.5
). This subsequently allows us to interpret the
n\times m
matrix
g(\Phi_{0})
as a Nemytskii operator, i.e., we proceed via the pointwise multiplication
g(\Phi_{0})[\xi](\textbf{x})=g(\Phi_{0}(\textbf{x}))\xi(\textbf{x})
, for any
\xi\in\mathcal{W}_{Q}
.

If
Q
is of trace class, i.e., Tr
(Q)=\|Q^{1/2}\|_{HS(L^{2};H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}^{2}<\infty,
then it suffices to demand

\sup_{\textbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}}|g(\Phi_{0}(\textbf{x}))|<\infty,

(1.7)

which is likely to hold in many situations, since it is supposed that the non-linearity
g
vanishes at the endpoints of the wave. This is also true if we replace
\mathcal{D}
by
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. However, for our setting (
1.2
) in combination with the fact that our domain is unbounded, we see that
the convolution operator
Q
, which acts as
Qv=q*v
,
cannot be of trace class (even for
d=1
; see
[
30
]
). The computations in §
4.1
show that the
well-posedness condition for (
1.6
) becomes

\|g(\Phi_{0})\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n\times m})}<\infty.

(1.8)

Note that for
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
this condition holds if and only if
d=1
,
since
\Phi_{0}
depends only on the first spatial coordinate. Hence, we consider domains of the form
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
, where (
1.8
) does hold for dimensions
d\geq 2.

The second main reason is that on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
d\geq 2,
one may only expect algebraic decay of perturbations
[
38
]
. In addition, if the disturbance is not sufficiently “localised” then one need not have any decay at all
[
5
]
. One therefore needs to carefully balance the technical requirements for the noise term with the machinery necessary to handle the slowly decaying terms. Scalar noise for instance, also known as spatially homogeneous noise (take
\mathcal{W}=\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
Q
a positive semi-definite matrix as in the previous works
[
27
,
28
]
), is of trace class but simply reduces the problem back to the one-dimensional case and hence generates no decay in the transverse direction.

We do intend to investigate the impact of coloured noise on travelling waves evolving on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the future, and the present paper can be seen as a preparatory study. For example,
our work here can be used to extract detailed information
concerning the stochastic behaviour of the phase and the dependence on the size
|\mathbb{T}|
of the torus.
We envision that the translational invariance
with respect to the transverse direction
will need to be loosened, utilising
localised or weighted noise as interesting alternatives.

Main result

We prove that a spectrally stable planar wave
on
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}

survives in a suitable sense under the influence of the small multiplicative noise terms in (
1.1
) or (
1.3
).
This is achieved by analysing perturbations of the form

v(x,y,t)=u(x+\gamma(t),y,t)-\Phi_{\sigma}(x),

(1.9)

and following the spirit of the procedure developed in
[
27
,
30
]
. We extend the pair
(\Phi_{0},c_{0})
to a branch of so-called instantaneous stochastic waves
(\Phi_{\sigma},c_{\sigma})

that satisfy
2
2

2

Throughout this paper, we shall often use the abbreviations
L^{2}=L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
H^{k}=H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. At times we also need to consider other domains and codomains, in which case we will always be explicit to prevent any confusion.

\|\Phi_{\sigma}-\Phi_{0}\|_{H^{k}}+|c_{\sigma}-c_{0}|=\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}),

(1.10)

which only feel stochastic forcing at onset.
The phase shift
\gamma(t)
is intended
to
stochastically “freeze” the solution
u
by
enforcing the orthogonality condition

\langle v(t),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,

(1.11)

for some function
\psi_{\rm tw}
related the adjoint of operator associated to the linearisation about the wave.
In particular, the phase shift
\gamma(t)
satisfies the stochastic (ordinary) differential equation

\mathrm{d}\gamma=\big[c_{\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(\|u(t)-\Phi_{\sigma}(\cdot-\gamma(t))\|_{H^{k}})\big]\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{O}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q},

(1.12)

and can, loosely speaking, be seen as the
“location” of the wave after spatially averaging over the
transverse coordinates. For a rigorous construction and more information, we refer to §
2
.

We are interested in
the probabilistic behaviour of the exit-time

t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)=\inf\{t\geq 0:\|v(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|v(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s>\eta\},

(1.13)

which measures at what time the solution
u(t)
deviates too much
from the expected
“location” of the profile
\Phi_{\sigma}
.
By taking
k>d/2
and using a standard Sobolev embedding,
the first term ensures that the pointwise size of
v(t)
stays under control until this stopping time. The second term (with a small regularisation parameter
\varepsilon>0
) provides integrated control over higher derivatives.
This is an important bonus feature of our method and can be seen as a type of optimal regularity result. For example, this enables us to provide
H^{1}
-results in dimensions
2\leq d\leq 4
, where no pointwise control is available. In addition, it could be exploited to consider nonlinearities
f
that include a dependence on the first derivatives
\nabla u
.

Our metastability result below not only improves upon the main findings presented in
[
29
]
, but also extends these to higher spatial dimensions. In particular, we may take
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}
when
d=1
(see Table
2.1
),
and the probability bound in (
1.14
) is now in line with those found in the context of large deviations theory
[
11
,
86
,
90
,
96
]
.
In words, the theorem states that with probability exponentially close to one,
our exit-time is
exponentially long with respect to the parameter
1/\sigma
.

Theorem 1.1

(Interpretation of Theorem
2.6
)
.

Under certain technical assumptions, planar fronts and pulses in stochastically perturbed reaction-diffusion equations evolving over
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
persist on exponentially long times scales. In particular,
there exists a constant
0<\mu<1
so that for all sufficiently small
\sigma>0
there is a stochastic scalar process
\gamma(t)
such that

\mathbb{P}(t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)<T)\leq 2T\exp\left(-\frac{\mu\eta}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

(1.14)

holds for any integer
T\geq 2
,
any sufficiently small exit value
\eta>0
, and any
initial value
u(0)
that satisfies
\|u(0)-\Phi_{\sigma}\|_{H^{k}}^{2}<\mu\eta
.

In one spatial dimension, several distinct approaches have been developed to study the behaviour of patterns under stochastic perturbations, using various alternative definitions
for the stochastic phase

[
3
,
34
,
47
,
52
,
53
,
60
,
87
,
88
]
.
An alternative technique that also leads to stability results on exponentially long timescales uses renormalisation to reset the phase tracking mechanism at suitable time points

[
14
,
22
,
47
,
63
]
. In two dimensions, the latter approach has been used to study rotating spirals
[
50
]
on a compact spatial domain. As we will explain in the sequel, our spatially unbounded setting introduces complications that are not covered by these results.

Local existence and uniqueness

While rigorous results on the impact of stochastic forcing on deterministic patterns are still relatively scarce
[
49
]
, general existence and uniqueness results for SPDEs have been well-developed over the past decades; see
[
8
,
10
,
11
,
16
,
21
,
24
,
25
,
33
,
35
,
73
,
100
]
and the references therein. Nevertheless,
in order to be able to talk about
H^{k}
-valued (local) variational solutions of (
1.1
), we need to exploit developments that are quite recent.

In spatial dimension
d=1
it has been shown—after substitution of
u=z+\Phi_{\rm ref}
into system (
1.1
) with
\Phi_{\rm ref}=\Phi_{0}
—that

\mathrm{d}z=[\partial_{x}^{2}z+\Phi_{\rm ref}^{\prime\prime}+f(z+\Phi_{\rm ref})]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma g(z+\Phi_{\rm ref})\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q}

(1.15)

admits a global unique solution
[
27
,
30
]
, under suitable conditions on the nonlinearities
f
and
g
. In particular, it crucially involves the
sign condition

\langle f(u)-f(v),u-v\rangle\leq K|u-v|^{2}

(1.16)

to control the super-linear terms in
f
; consider, e.g., the Allen-Cahn nonlinearity
f(u)=u-u^{3}
. This can be used to derive local monotonicity properties
such as

\displaystyle\langle f(\Phi+v_{A})-f(\Phi+v_{B}),v_{A}-v_{B}\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq K\|v_{A}-v_{B}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(1.17)

which should be contrasted with the estimate
3
3

3

This follows directly from the proof of
[
27
, Lem. 2.3.1]
, with the slight modification that instead of invoking the Sobolev embedding
\|u\|_{\infty}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{1}}
, we use the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality
\|u\|_{\infty}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{1/2}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{1/2}
; see
[
57
,
67
]
.

\displaystyle\|f(\Phi+v_{A})-f(\Phi+v_{B})\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\big(1+\|v_{A}\|_{L^{2}}\|v_{A}\|_{H^{1}}+\|v_{B}\|_{L^{2}}\|v_{B}\|_{H^{1}}\big)\|v_{A}-v_{B}\|_{L^{2}},

(1.18)

that is available for general cubic nonlinearities.
To be more precise, this global solution fits into the classical variational framework as described in Liu and Röckner
[
58
]
, which uses the Gelfand triple
(H^{1},L^{2},H^{-1})
to embed the SPDE and its solution
and relies heavily on these monotonicity properties.
For additive noise, these
conditions have been weakend by the same authors to
allow cross terms as in (
1.18
)
[
59
, Sec. 5.2]
. Further refinements are also possible
in the setting where the Gelfand triple embeddings
are compact
[
77
]
, yet both these extensions do not apply to our spatially extended system
with multiplicative noise.

The key issue is that our stability arguments require pointwise control over the perturbation
v(t)
, requiring us to work in
H^{k}
with
k>d/2
. Due to the fact that derivative terms such as
f(u)^{\prime}=f^{\prime}(u)u^{\prime}
automatically involve products, the
monotonicity property (
1.17
) fails to hold in
H^{k}
, even if the sign condition (
1.16
) is satisfied. Recently, a new variational framework by Agresti and Veraar
[
4
]
, the so-called critical variational framework, has been developed which basically replaces local monotonicity assumptions by local Lipschitz conditions. Our approach here is hybrid: we first
construct a global solution with respect to

(H^{1},L^{2},H^{-1})

and then apply
the results in
[
4
]

to conclude that these global solutions exist locally in
(H^{k+1},H^{k},H^{k-1})
. For convenience, we impose global
Lipschitz-smoothness on the nonlinearities in a
pointwise
fashion, which can readily be obtained by multiplying any sufficiently smooth nonlinearity with a smooth cut-off function. However, we caution the reader that this does
not
carry over to the nonlinearities viewed as operators on the relevant function spaces.
In §
5.3
we also explain how cubic nonlinearities can be accomodated in dimensions
1\leq d\leq 4.

Mild formulation

After performing some computations, we conclude that the perturbation
v(t)
is a variational solution
to
an initial value problem of the form

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}v(t)&=[A\big(t,v(t)\big)v(t)+F(t,v(t))]\mathrm{d}t+B(t,v(t))\mathrm{d}W^{Q}_{t},\\
v(0)&=v_{0},\end{cases}

(1.19)

in which the unbounded operator-valued function
A
now also carries a path-dependence that cannot be transformed away, in contrast to
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
]
where
d=1
. Nevertheless, we want to
pass to a mild formulation of the form

v(t)=E(t,0,\omega)v_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s,\omega)F(s,v(s))\mathrm{d}s+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s,\omega)B(s,v(s))\mathrm{d}W^{Q}_{s}}_{\textnormal{Q: How to interpret this integral?}},

(1.20)

to exploit the stability of the linearised flow encoded in the evolution family
E(t,s)
. Through the parameter
\omega\in\Omega
this family depends explicitly on the
probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, which should be seen as a direct consequence of the fact that
A
depends on
v(t)
. Consequently, the integrand
E(t,s)B(s,v(s))
is anticipating, and hence we cannot make sense of the stochastic integral in (
1.20
) with the usual Itô calculus. Mild formulations where
E(t,s)
is independent of
\omega\in\Omega
have been studied thoroughly in the survey
[
69
]
. In the previous papers

[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
]
it was possible to write

E(t,s)=S_{\rm tw}(t-s)
, using the semigroup
S_{\rm tw}(t)
associated to the linearisation of the travelling wave, for which general theory has been available for some time
[
11
,
21
,
24
]
.

The Skorokhod integral is one of the most well-studied extensions of the Itô integral to a certain set of anticipating integrands
[
85
]
. However, this is known to be inconvenient when studying mild solutions to parabolic equations with a random family of operators, primarily due to the introduction of a drift and the reliance
on so-called Mallivian derivatives which results in technical assumptions that cannot be easily verified; see

[
55
]
and
[
70
, Rem. 6.7]
.

Forward integrals

In our setting it turns out to be appropriate to use so-called forward integrals,
introduced by Russo and Vallois
[
79
,
80
,
81
]
.
In the operator-valued setting,
Léon and Nualart
[
55
]
were the first (and one of the few) to study forward integrals
and apply them to stochastic evolution equations.
Their existence and uniqueness results have been obtained by linking the forward integral back to the Skorokhod integral,
which transfers the associated inconveniences.
Recent progress has been made through the observation

[
75
, eq. (5.4)]
that links
these integrals to the theory of pathwise mild solutions as developed by Pronk and Veraar
[
74
]
;
see for example
[
70
, Sec. 6]

and the work of Kuehn and Neamţu
[
51
]
on stochastically perturbed quasilinear problems.

In §
6.3
we will show that the variational solution
v(t)

to (
1.19
)
satisfies the mild formulation

v(t)=E(t,0,\omega)v_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s,\omega)F(s,v(s))\mathrm{d}s+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s,\omega)B(s,v(s))\mathrm{d}W^{-}_{s}}_{\textnormal{A: As a forward integral!}}

(1.21)

involving a forward integral. To appreciate
this concept, let us consider
a standard Brownian motion
\beta=(\beta(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
Formally, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\mathrm{d}\beta(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\frac{\mathrm{d}\beta(s)}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\lim_{h\searrow 0}\frac{\beta(s+h)-\beta(s)}{h}\mathrm{d}s

(1.22)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\searrow 0}\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{t}g(s)[\beta(s+h)-\beta(s)]\mathrm{d}s,

which motivates the definition

\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\mathrm{d}\beta^{-}(s):=\lim_{n\to\infty}n\int_{0}^{t}g(s)[\beta(s+1/n)-\beta(s)]\mathrm{d}s

(1.23)

for the forward integral, interpreting the limit
in the ucp-topology. The intermediate steps in (
1.22
) do not make sense, since a Brownian motion is nowhere differentiable, yet we do have the identity
\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\mathrm{d}\beta(s)=\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\mathrm{d}\beta^{-}(s)
for adapted processes
g

[
81
, Prop 1.1]
.
Also, anticipating processes
g
exist for which the forward integral is well-defined, implying that it is indeed a proper extension of the Itô integral.
In the same spirit,
Russo and Vallois also studied so-called backward and symmetric integrals, which are extensions of the backward Itô and the Stratonovich integral, respectively.

Maximal inequalities for stochastic convolutions (a type of Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequalities
[
41
,
76
]
) play an important role in the series
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
]
. These inequalities are also essential in the current paper, now underlying both the well-posedness of our mild formulation and our stability analysis. For a general random
C_{0}
-evolution family
E(t,s)=E(t,s,\omega)
of contractions, one can use
[
70
, Thm. 6.4]
to obtain bounds of the form

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W^{-}_{s}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq K^{2p}p^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p},

(1.24)

which hold for any
p\geq 1
and some
K>0
independent of time
T
. This
recent result sharpens the earlier bounds obtained by
Léon and Nualart employing a factorisation method

[
55
, Thm. 4.4]
.

One of the contributions of this paper is that
we derive estimates for forward integrals
that work for certain
C_{0}
-evolution families that are only eventually contractive, i.e., satisfy the bound
\|E(t,s)\|_{H^{k}\to H^{k}}\leq M
with
M>1
.
To achieve this, we exploit the fact that the evolution families we encounter
can be decomposed into a family of contractions and the semigroup associated to the linearisation about the wave in one spatial dimension,
which admits
H^{\infty}
-calculus after projecting out the translational eigenfunction.
For certain one-component systems, it is known that the linearised flow is immediately contractive in the direction orthogonal to the translational eigenfunction. This is exploited by many authors in order to prove (nonlinear) stabilty results in stochastically perturbed systems
[
34
,
52
,
53
,
87
,
88
]
, but generally speaking it is not true or unclear whether the semigroup associated to the linearisation about the wave is contractive
[
27
,
99
]
. In addition, we provide novel maximal regularity estimates for these forward integrals, allowing us to control their
H^{k+1}
-norm in an integrated sense. This is a subtle task due to the delicate limiting process underlying their definition.
In any case, several of our results generalise naturally to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and are also applicable to other settings that involve random evolution families.

Expansions in
\sigma

One of the main features of our freezing method
is that the fluctuations around the
instantaneous stochastic wave
(\Phi_{\sigma},c_{\sigma})

can be readily investigated by expanding our
equations for
v(t)
and
\gamma(t)
in powers of
\sigma
.
For example, we may follow
[
30
]
to find the expansions

\displaystyle\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=c_{0}t+\sigma\gamma_{1}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}),\qquad c_{\sigma}=c_{0}+\sigma^{2}c_{0;2}+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{4}),

(1.25)

with the explicit expressions

\gamma_{1}(t)=-\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\int_{0}^{t}\langle g(\Phi_{0})\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

(1.26)

and

\displaystyle c_{0;2}

\displaystyle=-\,\frac{1}{2|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\langle\Phi_{0}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\langle g(\Phi_{0})Qg(\Phi_{0})^{\top}\psi_{\rm tw},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

(1.27)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\langle g(\Phi_{0})Qg(\Phi_{0})^{\top}\psi_{\rm tw},\psi_{\rm tw}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}-\langle h(\Phi_{0}),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

As before, the function
h
denotes the Itô-Stratonovich correction term. Also note that

\displaystyle\textrm{Var}\big[\gamma_{1}(t)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1})^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\langle g(\Phi_{0})\mathrm{d}W^{Q}_{s},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\right]^{2}

(1.28)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\langle g(\Phi_{0})Qg(\Phi_{0})^{\top}\psi_{\rm tw},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}t.

To illustrate the effects of the dimensions transverse to the propagation of the wave, we simply take
m=n=1
and
consider a convolution
Qv=q*v
whose kernel
q

can be factorised as follows:
q(x,y)=q_{\rm wv}(x)q_{\perp}(y)
for
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}
.
Writing
Q_{\rm wv}v=q_{\rm wv}*v
for the one-dimensional convolution operator along the wave direction yields

Qg(\Phi_{0})^{\top}\psi_{\rm tw}=\mathfrak{q}_{\rm avg}|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}Q_{\rm wv}g(\Phi_{0})\psi_{\rm tw},\qquad\mathfrak{q}_{\rm avg}=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}q_{\perp}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

(1.29)

In addition, we define

h(u)=\frac{\mu}{2}q(0)g^{\prime}(u)g(u)
for
\mu\in\{0,1\}
and recall that
\mu=0
corresponds with the Itô interpretation (
1.1
) while
\mu=1

encodes the Stratonovich interpretation (
1.3
).
These choices allow us to find

\displaystyle\textrm{Var}\big[\gamma_{1}(t)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}_{\rm avg}\langle g(\Phi_{0})Q_{\rm wv}g(\Phi_{0})\psi_{\rm tw},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}t

(1.30)

together with

\displaystyle c_{0;2}

\displaystyle=-\,\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{q}_{\rm avg}\langle\Phi_{0}^{\prime\prime},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\langle g(\Phi_{0})Q_{\rm wv}g(\Phi_{0})\psi_{\rm tw},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}

(1.31)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{q}_{\rm avg}\langle g(\Phi_{0})Q_{\rm wv}g(\Phi_{0})\psi_{\rm tw},\psi_{\rm tw}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}-\frac{\mu}{2}\langle g^{\prime}(\Phi_{0})g(\Phi_{0}),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}.

To recover the one-dimensional results in
[
30
, Sec. 2.3]
, one simply sets
q_{\perp}\equiv 1
which implies
\mathfrak{q}_{\rm avg}=1
, independent of the size of the torus
\mathbb{T}.

Indeed, this choice models spatially homogeneous noise in the transverse direction.
The situation changes however if the noise correlation decays in the
y
-direction; for example, take a kernel
q_{\perp}
that is supported on
[-1,1]
and does not depend on the value
|\mathbb{T}|
. In this case one has
\mathfrak{q}_{\rm avg}\to 0
as
|\mathbb{T}|\to\infty
. Consequently,
we see that

\mathrm{Var}\big[\gamma_{1}(t)\big]\to 0,\qquad c_{0;2}\to-\frac{\mu}{2}\langle g^{\prime}(\Phi_{0})g(\Phi_{0}),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(1.32)

The vanishing variance of
\gamma_{1}(t)
can intuitively be appreciated by noting that the energy inserted by the noise can be dissipated in the transverse direction rather than causing fluctuations in the phase of the wave. This seems to suggests
that on the whole space
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
one can proceed in the spirit of the deterministic approach
[
39
]
and replace the
global phase function by local phase functions.

Under appropriate parameter regimes, the examples in
[
30
]
displayed a change
of sign for
c_{0;2}
when switching between

\mu=0
and
\mu=1
(i.e.,
\mathfrak{q}_{\rm avg}=1
). Now, in the Stratonovich setting
\mu=1
,
it is conceivable that
c_{0;2}
changes sign
as the parameter
|\mathbb{T}|
is adjusted between 0 and infinity. This leads to a potential explanation for the dimension-dependent speed changes observed
in
[
84
]
; recall the discussion above
in the context of (
1.4
).
We intend to examine this further in a forthcoming paper, which will include higher order expansions that are able to capture curvature-driven effects. This will guide the development of an appropriate theory for the full case
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Organisation

The structure of this paper is outlined as follows. In §
2
we formulate our assumptions, state our main results and provide an overview of the main steps in the proof. In §
3
we study random evolution families, introduce the concept of the forward integral, and obtain key estimates and regularity results for the associated stochastic convolutions. In §
4
we provide bounds for our nonlinearities, which we use
in §
5
to construct global and local solutions to our problem. In §
6
we work towards a mild representation for the perturbation
v(t)
,
for which we then provide stability estimates in §
7
.

2
Main results

In this section we state our findings for the existence and metastability of planar wave solutions to stochastic reaction-diffusion systems of the form

\mathrm{d}u=[D\Delta u+f(u)+\sigma^{2}h(u)]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma g(u)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q},

(2.1)

where
u(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
evolves in time
t\geq 0
on a cylindrical domain
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\ni(x,y)
with dimension
d\geq 2
, and is driven by a translationally invariant noise process
(W^{Q}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. The Laplacian acts in a standard fashion on the spatial coordinates
(x,y)
as

\Delta u=\partial_{x}^{2}u+\Delta_{y}u,

(2.2)

and the diffusion matrix
D
is diagonal with strictly positive diagonal elements. In particular, we will denote a derivative with respect to

x
interchangeably by

\partial_{x}
and a prime
′
. Note that throughout this work, we also allow
d=1
with
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 2.1
.

To improve the readability of our arguments,
we will assume that
D=I_{n}
from this point forward,
where
I_{n}
is the
n\times n
identity matrix.
Indeed, the approach used in
[
28
,
30
]
to handle varying constants on the diagonal also works in the present multidimensional setting.

To set the stage, we impose several conditions on the nonlinearity
f
and also the deterministic planar wave that travels in the
x
-direction. In §
2.2
, we formulate conditions on the noise term and the Itô-Stratonovich correction term
h
, guaranteeing the existence and uniqueness of solutions in various variational settings. In §
2.3
, we couple an extra SDE to the SPDE above that will serve as a phase-tracking mechanism.
This enables us to formulate and discuss our main stability results.

2.1
Deterministic setup

We start by formulating our conditions for the nonlinearity
f
, which involve an arbitrary integer
k\geq 0
that varies depending on the context. This parameter is associated to the degree of smoothness (in the
H^{k}
-sense) that we can expect our solutions to have. Our most general result that is valid in arbitrary dimensions
d\geq 1
will require
k>d/2
and hence provide pointwise control. In this case, we need to impose global Lipschitz conditions on
f
and its derivatives. We do point out that the pointwise control in our final stability result means that we can safely modify our nonlinearities outside the region of interest to enforce these conditions.

(Hf-Lip)

We have
f\in C^{k+2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
f(u_{-})=f(u_{+})=0
for some pair
u_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
In addition, there is a constant
K_{f}>0
such that

|f(u_{A})-f(u_{B})|+\ldots+|D^{k+1}f(u_{A})-D^{k+1}f(u_{B})|\leq K_{f}|u_{A}-u_{B}|

(2.3)

holds for all
u_{A},u_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In lower dimensions
1\leq d\leq 4
it is also possible to consider solutions (and noise) with a lower degree of smoothness. In fact, we will be able to take
k=1
and allow our nonlinearity
f
to have cubic growth. Observe that for
2\leq d\leq 4
this can no longer be artifically imposed by modifying
f
since we no longer have pointwise control over our
H^{1}
-valued solutions.

(Hf-Cub)

We have
1\leq d\leq 4
and
f\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
f(u_{-})=f(u_{+})=0
for some pair
u_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In addition, there exists a constant
K_{f}>0
so that the bound

|D^{3}f(u)|\leq K_{f}

(2.4)

holds for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

We assume the existence of a planar wave solution
u(x,y,t)=\Phi_{0}(x-c_{0}t)
that approaches its limits
u_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

at an exponential rate; a common assumption connected to asymptotic hyperbolicity
[
39
,
82
]
that holds in many applications.
Together with (Hf-Lip) or (Hf-Cub), this condition implies that

\Phi_{0}\in C^{k+4}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
holds together with

|\Phi_{0}^{(\ell)}(x)|\to 0
exponentially fast as
|x|\to\infty
, for any
1\leq\ell\leq k+4
,
showing that
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}\in H^{k+3}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(HTw)

There exists a waveprofile
\Phi_{0}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and a wavespeed
c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}

that satisfy the travelling wave ODE

\Phi_{0}^{\prime\prime}+c_{0}\Phi_{0}^{\prime}+f(\Phi_{0})=0.

(2.5)

In addition, there is a constant
K>0
together with exponents
\nu_{\pm}>0
so that the bound

|\Phi_{0}(x)-u_{-}|+|\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(\xi)|\leq Ke^{-\nu_{-}|x|}

(2.6)

holds for all
x\leq 0,
whereas the bound

|\Phi_{0}(x)-u_{+}|+|\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(\xi)|\leq Ke^{-\nu_{+}|x|}

(2.7)

holds for all
x\geq 0.

Linearising (
2.5
) around the travelling wave
(\Phi_{0},c_{0})
leads to the linear operator

\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}:H^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})

(2.8)

that acts as

[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}u](x)=u^{\prime\prime}(x)+c_{0}u^{\prime}(x)+Df(\Phi_{0}(x))u(x).

(2.9)

In particular, observe
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}\Phi_{0}^{\prime}=0
. Its associated adjoint operator, which we will denote by

\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj}:H^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}),

(2.10)

acts as

[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj}w](x)=w^{\prime\prime}(x)-c_{0}w^{\prime}(x)+Df(\Phi_{0}(x))w(x),

(2.11)

and it is easily verified that
\langle\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}v,w\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle v,\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj}w\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
holds for
v,w\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}).

We continue with a standard spectral stability condition
for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
. In particular,
we demand that the translational eigenvalue at zero is an isolated simple eigenvalue and that the remainder of the spectrum can be strictly bounded to the left of the imaginary axis, i.e., there is a spectral gap.

(HS)

The operator
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}:H^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
has a simple eigenvalue at
\lambda=0
and there exists a constant
\beta>0
so that the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}-\lambda:H^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is invertible for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying Re
\lambda\geq-2\beta
.

Assuming (Hf-Lip) or (Hf-Cub) together with (HTw) and (HS),
we note that the operators
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj}
map
H^{\ell+2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
into
H^{\ell}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for any
0\leq\ell\leq k+1
.
This follows either directly from inspection or general (Sobolev tower) interpolation theory
[
15
,
62
,
93
]
.
Furthermore, the resolvent set of
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
restricted to the domain
H^{k+3}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
contains the resolvent set of
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
seen as the original operator with domain
H^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Consequently, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}:H^{k+3}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to H^{k+1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is Fredholm with index zero, we have

\ker(\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw})=\text{span}\{\Phi_{0}^{\prime}\}\subset H^{k+3}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\ker(\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj})=\text{span}\{\psi_{\rm tw}\}\subset H^{k+3}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}),

(2.12)

for some
\psi_{\rm tw}
that satisfies the normalised identity

\langle\Phi_{0}^{\prime},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=1,

(2.13)

and
|\psi_{\rm tw}^{(\ell)}(x)|\to 0
exponentially fast as
|x|\to\infty
for any
0\leq\ell\leq k+3
.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
is a lower order perturbation of the diffusion operator
\partial_{x}^{2}
, we see that

\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
is sectorial
4
4

4

Following a definition as in
[
32
, Sec. 10]
, we have that
-\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
is sectorial.
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
H^{k+1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
(the latter two after restriction), and hence generates analytic semigroups
on these spaces, which we all denote by
(S_{\rm tw}(t))_{t\geq 0}
since they agree where they overlap
[
15
,
62
,
93
]
.
The same conclusions hold for the adjoint operator
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj}
.

Lastly, the orthogonal projection
P_{\rm tw}:H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, defined by

P_{\rm tw}v=\langle v,\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\Phi_{0}^{\prime},

(2.14)

plays a crucial role for our stability analysis. It is used to project out the translational eigenfunction in order to circumvent the neutral mode of the semigroup
S_{\mathrm{tw}}(t)
.

2.2
Stochastic setup

In this part we formulate conditions to ensure that the stochastic terms in (
2.1
) are well-defined.
We start by considering the covariance function
q

that governs the noise process,
writing
\hat{q}
for the Fourier transform of
q
(see also Appendix
C
).
Recall, the integer
m
corresponds
to the dimension of the space from which the noise will be sampled.

(Hq)

We have
q\in H^{\ell}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})\cap L^{1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})
for some integer
\ell>2k+d/2,
with
q(-\mathbf{x})=q(\mathbf{x})
and
q^{\top}(\mathbf{x})=q(\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{x}=(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
. Further, for
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\omega,\xi)\in\widehat{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
the
m\times m
matrix
\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
is non-negative definite.

Thanks to Young’s inequality, the integrability of the kernel
q
allows us to introduce the bounded convolution operator
Q
on
L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})
that acts as

[Qv](\mathbf{x})=[q*v](\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathcal{D}}q(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})v(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}q(x-x^{\prime},y-y^{\prime})v(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}.

(2.15)

The properties in (Hq) imply that
Q
is symmetric and that
\langle Qv,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})}\geq 0
holds for every
v\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})
, showing that
Q
is indeed a covariance operator. Consequently, we can follow
[
11
,
21
,
24
,
30
,
42
,
73
]
and construct a cylindrical
Q
-Wiener process
W^{Q}=(W^{Q}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
that is defined on a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})

and takes values in (an extended space containing) the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})
. For more information, we refer to §
3.2
.

In order to perform
H^{k}
-valued stochastic integration with respect to
W^{Q}
, we need to interpret the noise term
g(u)

in (
2.1
) as a Hilbert-Schmidt operator from
L^{2}_{Q}
into
H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
in which we have introduced the Hilbert space

L_{Q}^{2}=Q^{1/2}\big(L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})\big).

(2.16)

We proceed by viewing
g
as a Nemytskii operator that
acts in a pointwise fashion as

(g(u)[\xi])(\mathbf{x})=g(u(\mathbf{x}))\xi(\mathbf{x}),

(2.17)

for any
\xi\in L^{2}_{Q}
. We impose the following pointwise conditions on
g
, which most notably require
g
to vanish at the limits
u_{\pm}
for the waveprofile.

(HSt)

We have
g\in C^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times m})
with
g(u_{-})=g(u_{+})=0.
In addition, there is a constant
K_{g}>0
such that

|g(u_{A})-g(u_{B})|+\ldots+|D^{k+1}g(u_{A})-D^{k+1}g(u_{B})|\leq K_{g}|u_{A}-u_{B}|

(2.18)

holds for all
u_{A},u_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

The results in §
4.1

use the
H^{\ell}
-smoothness of
q
to show that
g(u)

has the desired properties for all
u
in the affine spaces

\mathcal{U}_{H^{k}(S;\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\Phi_{\rm ref}+H^{k}(S;\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad S\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathcal{D}\},

(2.19)

where
\Phi_{\rm ref}
is a sufficiently smooth reference function that has
\Phi_{\rm ref}(\pm\infty)=u_{\pm}
; for example
\Phi_{\rm ref}=\Phi_{0}
.
Indeed, in general we cannot work directly with
H^{k}
since the wave
\Phi_{0}
is not included in this space.
On the other hand, in the special case
u_{+}=u_{-}=0
one can simply take
\Phi_{\rm ref}=0
.

The purpose of the function
h
in (
2.1
) is to allow Itô-Stratonovich correction
terms to be incorporated into our framework. For example, in the scalar case
n=m=1
the choice

h(u)=\frac{1}{2}q(0)g^{\prime}(u)g(u)
allows us to interpret (
2.1
) as the Stratonovich SPDE

du=[\Delta u+f(u)]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma g(u)\circ\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q}.

(2.20)

We refer to

[
16
,
92
,
94
]

for further information. In view of the example above, we require one order of smoothness less on
h
as compared to
g
.

(HCor)

We have
h\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
h(u_{-})=h(u_{+})=0
. In addition, there is a constant
K_{h}>0
such that

|h(u_{A})-h(u_{B})|+\ldots+|D^{k}h(u_{A})-D^{k}h(u_{B})|\leq K_{h}|u_{A}-u_{B}|

(2.21)

holds for all
u_{A},u_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

2.3
Overview

Our goal here is to provide a
comprehensive overview of the key steps and
intermediate results that lead up to Theorem
2.6
.
In particular, it will become clear in what sense our solutions are defined, how our phase-tracking mechanism is implemented, and which expectation estimates tend towards the main (meta)stability estimate.
Our results cover different combinations of the parameters
(k,d)
, as summarised in Table
2.1
.

non-linearity
f

\geq

2

local

yes

Table 2.1:
List of compatible combinations of the smoothness parameter
k
and dimension
d
, with (meta)stability on exponentially long time scales.

: The case (Hf-Cub) with
k=0
and
d=1
is treated in
[

]
and requires the one-sided inequality (
1.16
) as an additional assumption.

: (meta)stability for the case

. In particular, the techniques in this paper can be used to strengthen the bound obtained in
[

]
.

Step 1: Phase-tracking mechanism

Let us continue the discussion from the introduction.
We couple the SDE

\mathrm{d}\gamma=[c+a_{\sigma}(u,\gamma;c)]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma b(u,\gamma)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q}

(2.22)

to the SPDE (
2.1
).
The two functions

a_{\sigma}:\mathcal{U}_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\quad\text{and}\quad b:\mathcal{U}_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\times\mathbb{R}\to HS(L^{2}_{Q};\mathbb{R})

(2.23)

are defined in Appendix
A
,
based on technical considerations to ensure
that our perturbation does not feel the neutral
translational mode of the linear flow;
see Step 3 for further information. The velocity
parameter
c
is discussed further in Step 4.

Step 2: Existence, uniqueness, and regularity

Our first result will clarify what we mean by a solution to system (
2.1
). In particular, under the assumption (Hf-Lip) we will
obtain continuous
L^{2}
-valued solutions
that are unique up to indistinguishability
[
76
]
.
As a matter of fact, there exists a version of
z(t)
, let us say
\tilde{z}(t),
which satisfies (i)-(iii) and for which the map
\tilde{z}:(0,T]\times\Omega\mapsto H^{1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is progressively measurable
[
4
,
59
,
73
]
. However
\tilde{z}(t)
is not continuous in
H^{1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We refer to §
5
for the precise interpretation of the diffusion operator in the integral equation (
2.26
), which should be seen in the context of Gelfand triples
[
17
, Sec. 5.9]
.

Proposition 2.2

(see §
5
)
.

Fix
k=0
and assume that

(Hf-Lip)
,
(HSt)
,
(HCor)
,

(HTw)
and
(Hq)
are satisfied.
Pick
T>0
together with
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
. Then for any initial condition

(z_{0},\gamma_{0})\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathbb{R},

(2.24)

there are progressively measurable maps

z:[0,T]\times\Omega\to L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\gamma:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R},

(2.25)

that satisfy the following properties:

(i)

For almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, the map

t\mapsto(z(t,\omega),\gamma(t,\omega))

is of class
C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathbb{R})
;

(ii)

We have the integrability condition

(z,\gamma)\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega,\mathrm{d}t\times\mathbb{P};H^{1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathbb{R})
;

(iii)

The
L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
-valued identity
5
5

5

At first, the equality in (
2.26
) should be understood as an equality in the dual space of
H^{1}
, thus in the analytically weak sense, but by (ii) we can conclude that we have equality in
L^{2}
. See also
[
27
]
,
[
59
, Rem. 4.2.2]
and §
5
for more information.

\displaystyle z(t)=z_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}[\Delta z(s)+\Phi_{\rm ref}^{\prime\prime}]\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}f(z(s)+\Phi_{\rm ref})\mathrm{d}s

(2.26)

\displaystyle+\sigma^{2}\int_{0}^{t}h(z(s)+\Phi_{\rm ref})\mathrm{d}s+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}g(z(s)+\Phi_{\rm ref})\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q},

together with the scalar identity

\gamma(t)=\gamma_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}[c+a_{\sigma}(z(s)+\Phi_{\rm ref},\gamma(s);c)]\mathrm{d}s+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}b(z(s)+\Phi_{\rm ref},\gamma(s))\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q},

(2.27)

hold
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
0\leq t\leq T
;

(iv)

Suppose there are another progressively measurable maps
\tilde{z}
and
\tilde{\gamma}
that satisfy
(i)–(iii)
. Then for almost all
\omega\in\Omega,
we have

\tilde{z}(t,\omega)=z(t,\omega)\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\gamma}(t,\omega)=\gamma(t,\omega),\quad\text{for all }0\leq t\leq T.

(2.28)

Upon fixing
k=1
and assuming
(Hf-Cub)
instead of
(Hf-Lip)
, the same results hold after replacing the pair
(L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}),H^{1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}))
by
(H^{1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}),H^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.

In §
5.2
we investigate the regularity of the solution described in Proposition
2.2
. We observe that the solution
z(t)
with a smooth initial condition
z_{0}\in H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
remains
H^{k}
-smooth on (at least) a short realisation-dependent time interval
[0,\tau(\omega))
, but could lose smoothness over time. An interesting corollary of our stability result in Step 5 is that, with very high probability, solutions remain smooth for a very long time.

Step 3: Evolution of the perturbation

For the purposes of this step, we may choose any pair
(\Phi,c)
that is sufficiently close to
(\Phi_{0},c_{0})

as the basis for our perturbation argument. In particular, we impose the following condition
and refer to Appendix
A
for more information.

(HPar)

The conditions (HTw) and (HS) hold and the pair
(\Phi,c)\in\mathcal{U}_{H^{k+2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\times\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\|\Phi-\Phi_{0}\|_{H^{k+2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\min\{1,|\mathbb{T}|^{\frac{d-1}{2}}[4\|\psi\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}]^{-1}\},\quad|c-c_{0}|\leq 1.

(2.29)

We now decompose
u(t)
as

u(t)=T_{\gamma(t)}\Phi+T_{\gamma(t)}v(t),

(2.30)

or equivalently, define the perturbation
v(t)
by writing

v(t)=T_{-\gamma(t)}u(t)-\Phi,

(2.31)

where
T_{\delta},
for any
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
, is the right-shift operator that act on the first coordinate only, i.e.,

T_{\delta}u(x,y,t)=u(x-\delta,y,t),\quad\delta\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.32)

The computations in §
6.1
exploit the translational invariance of our noise to show that
v(t)
satisfies the system

\mathrm{d}v=\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}(v;c,\Phi)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathcal{S}(v;\Phi)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q},

(2.33)

with the deterministic part being of the form

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}(v;c,\Phi)=\Delta_{y}v+\kappa_{\sigma}(\Phi+v,0)\big[\partial_{x}^{2}v+\Phi^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(\Phi+v,0;c)\big]+a_{\sigma}(\Phi+v,0;c)\partial_{x}(\Phi+v),

(2.34)

while the stochastic part reads

\mathcal{S}(v;\Phi)[\xi]=g(\Phi+v)[\xi]+\partial_{x}(\Phi+v)b(\Phi+v,0)[\xi],\quad\xi\in L^{2}_{Q}.

(2.35)

The exact definitions of the scalar function
\kappa_{\sigma}
and the nonlinearity
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}

are provided in Appendix
A
, yet the main take away is that these terms are (more or less) the same as in the
d=1
case
[
30
]
and do not involve second order derivatives. In addition, we have
\mathcal{R}_{0}(0,c_{0},\Phi_{0})=0
.

Our goal is to ensure that the orthogonality condition

\langle v(t),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0

(2.36)

is satisfied as long as possible, because
this causes the perturbation to not feel any of the dangerous neutral modes of our higher spatially dimensional system (see §
3.2
).
In order to achieve this, we set out to enforce the conditions

\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}(v;c,\Phi)},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0,

(2.37)

together with

\langle{\mathcal{S}(v;\Phi)[\xi]},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0,\quad\xi\in L_{Q}^{2},

(2.38)

which is possible whenever
\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
is sufficiently small
and by choosing the functions
a_{\sigma}
and
b

appropriately.
Indeed, condition (
2.37
) is satisfied whenever

\displaystyle a_{\sigma}(\Phi+v,0;c)=-\kappa_{\sigma}(\Phi+v,0)\frac{\langle\partial_{xx}v+\Phi^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(\Phi+v,0;c),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}}{\langle\partial_{x}(\Phi+v),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}},

(2.39)

which features only derivatives with respect to the coordinate in the direction of the wave. This is due to the fact that

\langle\Delta_{y}v,\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\rm tw}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\nabla_{y}v)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=0,

(2.40)

as a consequence of Gauss’ divergence theorem, or more simply due to the fact that
\nabla_{y}v
is periodic and because the integral of the derivative of a periodic function vanishes.
Likewise,
condition (
2.38
) is satisfied whenever

b(\Phi+v,0)[\xi]=-\frac{\langle g(\Phi+v)\xi,\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}}{\langle\partial_{x}(\Phi+v),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}},\quad\xi\in L^{2}_{Q}.

(2.41)

This briefly motivates the expressions for
a_{\sigma}
and
b
found in Appendix
A
, which require less regularity on
v

and also contain cut-off functions to account for the cases where
v
is not sufficiently small.

Step 4: Instantaneous stochastic waves

In this step we use our remaining degrees of freedom
to pick the initial phase
\gamma_{0}
from Step 2
and the profile
(\Phi,c)
appearing in Step 3.
To this end,
we construct a branch of profiles and speeds

(\Phi_{\sigma},c_{\sigma})
that turns out to be highly convenient for our computations and Taylor expansions.
In particular, we will demand

\Phi_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma},0;c_{\sigma})=0,

(2.42)

which by construction implies that

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}(0;c_{\sigma},\Phi_{\sigma})=0\quad\text{and}\quad a_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma},0;c_{\sigma})=0.

(2.43)

In particular, when
u(t)=T_{\gamma(t)}\Phi_{\sigma}
holds
for some
t
, the phase
\gamma(t)-c_{\sigma}t

and perturbation
v(t)
will instantaneously only
feel stochastic forcing, motivating their name.
Referring to Appendix
A
, we note that

\mathcal{J}_{0}(\Phi,0;c)=c\Phi^{\prime}+f(\Phi)
,
which in view of (
2.5
) implies that the branch
(\Phi_{\sigma},c_{\sigma})
reduces naturally
to the deterministic pair
(\Phi_{0},c_{0})
at
\sigma=0
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Suppose that either
(Hf-Lip)
is satisfied with
k\geq 0
or
that
k=1
and
(Hf-Cub)
is satisfied. Assume furthermore that

(Hq)
,
(HSt)
,
(HCor)
,
(HTw)
and
(HS)
hold and pick a sufficiently large
K>0
.
Then there exists a
\delta_{\sigma}>0
so that for every
0\leq\sigma\leq\delta_{\sigma}
there is a unique pair

(\Phi_{\sigma},c_{\sigma})\in\mathcal{U}_{H^{k+2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\times\mathbb{R}

(2.44)

that satisfies

\Phi_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma},0;c_{\sigma})=0

(2.45)

and admits the bound

\|\Phi_{\sigma}-\Phi_{0}\|_{H^{k+2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+|c_{\sigma}-c_{0}|\leq K\sigma^{2}.

(2.46)

Proof.

Note that this is a problem in one spatial dimension. In particular,
the result readily follows from a similar fixed point argument as in the proof of
[
27
, Prop. 2.2.2]
; see also
[
30
, Sec. 4.5]
.
∎

We are interested in solutions with an initial condition
u(0)=u_{0}
close to
\Phi_{\sigma}.
The result below shows that the initial phase
\gamma(0)=\gamma_{0}

can be chosen in such a way that the orthogonality condition (
2.36
) holds at
t=0
(and hence for all later times until we lose control over the size of
v
).

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose that either
(Hf-Lip)
is satisfied with
k\geq 0
or
that
k=1
and
(Hf-Cub)
is satisfied. Assume furthermore that

(Hq)
,
(HSt)
,
(HCor)
,
(HTw)
and
(HS)
all hold.
Then there exist constants
\delta_{0}>0,\delta_{\sigma}>0
and
K>0
so that for every
0\leq\sigma\leq\delta_{\sigma}
and any initial condition
u_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
that satisfies

\|u_{0}-\Phi_{\sigma}\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\delta_{0},

(2.47)

there exists a
\gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
for which the function

v_{\gamma_{0}}=T_{-\gamma_{0}}u_{0}-\Phi_{\sigma}

(2.48)

satisfies the orthogonality condition
\langle v_{\gamma_{0}},\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0

and admits the bound

|\gamma_{0}|+\|v_{\gamma_{0}}\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq K\|u_{0}-\Phi_{\sigma}\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(2.49)

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of
[
27
, Prop. 2.2.3]
.
∎

Step 5: Mild formulation

In order to utilise the stability properties of the semigroup
S_{\rm tw}(t)
associated to the one-dimensional travelling wave, we need to establish some form of mild
representation for the perturbation
v(t)
. Due to the
quasilinear structure of our problem, this turns out to be rather subtle. In §
6.2
-
6.3
we perform
a (stochastic) time transformation allowing
us to arrive at the representation
6
6

6

For notational clarity, we continue to use
t
for the transformed time in this section.

v(t)=E(t,0)v(0)+\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(v(s))\mathrm{d}s+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(v(s))\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}.

(2.50)

Observe that
E(t,s)
denotes the random evolution family generated
by the time-dependent family of linear operators

t\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(v(t)):=\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}+\kappa_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v(t),0)^{-1}\Delta_{y}.

(2.51)

In addition, the nonlinearities
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}
are defined in (
A.28
) and satisfy

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(v)&=&\kappa_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v,0)^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}(v;c_{\sigma},\Phi_{\sigma})-\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(v)v,\\[5.69046pt]
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(v)&=&\kappa_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v,0)^{-1/2}\mathcal{S}(v;\Phi_{\sigma}).\end{array}

(2.52)

We note that the
\kappa_{\sigma}
factors are generated by the time transformation.
Due to the dependence of the behaviour of the solution on the interval
[s,t]
, the function
s\mapsto E(t,s)
is no longer progressively measurable. In particular, the stochastic integral in (
2.50
) is a so-called forward integral, which we discuss at length in §
3.2
.

As explained in Appendix
A
, the alternative representation (
A.30
) can be used to conclude that also
\langle\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(v),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0

holds whenever
\|v\|_{L^{2}}
is small. Clearly, we also have
\langle\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(v),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0

whenever
\|v\|_{L^{2}}
is small. These orthogonality conditions are crucial to circumvent the neutral modes of the random evolution family
E(t,s),
resulting in long-time stability.

Step 6: Long-time stability

As explained in the introduction, our strategy to obtain a stability result for the pair
(\Phi_{\sigma},c_{\sigma})

is to control the size of the perturbation
v(t)
in terms of the expression

N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)=\|v(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|v(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

(2.53)

in which
\varepsilon>0
is a small fixed parameter.
For any
\eta>0
, the associated stopping time is given by

t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)=\inf\{t\geq 0:N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)>\eta\}.

(2.54)

The choice of
k
becomes important here, as we will need to achieve control over the nonlinear terms in
(
2.52
).
In more detail,
in §
4
we take
k>d/2
when (Hf-Lip) is assumed
and show that

\|\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(v)\|_{H^{k}}\leq K\|v\|_{H^{k}}^{2}(1+\|v\|_{H^{k+1}})+\sigma^{2}K\|v\|_{H^{k+1}}

(2.55)

holds for some
K>0
, whenever
\|v\|_{H^{k}}\leq 1
.
Alternatively, assuming (Hf-Cub), we obtain

\|\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(v)\|_{H^{1}}\leq K\|v\|_{H^{2}}^{2}+\sigma^{2}K(1+\|v\|_{H^{2}})\|v\|_{H^{2}},

(2.56)

whenever
\|v\|_{H^{1}}\leq 1
holds. In §
7
we shall use the fact that both cases satisfy

\|\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(v)\|_{H^{k+1}}\leq K\|v\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}+\sigma^{2}K\|v\|_{H^{k+1}},\quad\|v\|_{H^{k}}\leq 1,

(2.57)

for some appropriate value of
k
.

Our main result here shows how the expected
supremum of
N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)
behaves as we increase
T
.

Proposition 2.5

(see §
7
)
.

Suppose that either
(Hf-Lip)
is satisfied with
k>d/2
or
that
k=1
and
(Hf-Cub)
is satisfied. Assume furthermore that

(Hq)
,
(HSt)
,
(HCor)
,
(HTw)
and
(HS)
hold
and pick
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.
Then there exist constants
\delta_{\eta}>0
,
\delta_{\sigma}>0
, and
K>0
so that, for any integer
T\geq 2,
any
0<\eta<\delta_{\eta},
any
0\leq\sigma\leq\delta_{\sigma},
and any integer
p\geq 1
, we have the moment bound

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}|N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)|^{p}\right]\leq K^{p}\Bigg[\|v(0)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}+\sigma^{2p}(p^{p}+\log(T)^{p})\Bigg].

(2.58)

We conclude that we can choose the time
T
to be exponentially large in
1/\sigma.

More specifically, for any
0\leq T\leq\exp[\delta_{\sigma}^{2}/\sigma^{2}]
, the estimate above shows

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}|N_{\varepsilon,k}(t)|^{p}\right]\lesssim\|v(0)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}+\mathcal{O}(\delta_{\sigma}^{2p}).

(2.59)

The control
on all powers of
N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)
enables us to obtain the probability bound
(
2.60
), which is an improvement upon the result for
k=0
and
d=1
in
[
29
]
and in line with the estimates found in the literature regarding large deviations theory
[
11
,
86
,
90
,
96
]
. The proof is relatively direct, using
an exponential Markov-type inequality.

Theorem 2.6
.

Suppose that either
(Hf-Lip)
is satisfied with
k>d/2
or
that
k=1
and
(Hf-Cub)
is satisfied. Assume furthermore that

(Hq)
,
(HSt)
,
(HCor)
,
(HTw)
and
(HS)
hold
and pick
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.
Then there exist constants
0<\mu<1
,
\delta_{\eta}>0
, and
\delta_{\sigma}>0
such that, for any integer
T\geq 2,
any
0<\eta\leq\delta_{\eta}
, any
0\leq\sigma\leq\delta_{\sigma}
, and any initial value
u(0)\in\mathcal{U}_{H^{k}}
that satisfies
\|u(0)-\Phi_{\sigma}\|_{H^{k}}^{2}<\mu\eta
, we have

\mathbb{P}(t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)<T)\leq 2T\exp\left(-\frac{\mu\eta}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

(2.60)

Proof.

Introducing the random variable

Z_{T}=\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st(\eta;k)}\wedge T}|N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)|,

(2.61)

we observe that
\mathbb{P}(t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)<T)=\mathbb{P}(Z_{T}\geq\eta)
holds.
Using the bounds (
2.58
) for all integers
p
, we may apply the exponential Markov-type inequality in Lemma
B.1

with

\Theta_{1}=K\sigma^{2},\quad\Theta_{2}=K\|v(0)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+K\sigma^{2}\log(T),

(2.62)

to obtain

\mathbb{P}(Z_{T}\geq\eta)\leq 3T^{1/2e}\exp\left(-\frac{\eta-2eK\|v(0)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}}{2eK\sigma^{2}}\right).

(2.63)

The bound (
2.60
) now readily follows upon choosing
\mu\leq(4eK)^{-1}
and by noting that
3T^{1/2e}\leq 2T
holds for
T\geq 2
.
∎

Finally, we remark that the control of the second moment
(p=1)

in (
2.58
) alone already allows us to show that the exit probability increases logarithmically in time. Indeed,
applying the standard Markov inequality
to the random variable (
2.61
)
yields

\mathbb{P}(t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)<T)=\mathbb{P}(Z_{T}\geq\eta)\leq\eta^{-1}\mathbb{E}Z_{T}\leq\eta^{-1}K\left[\|u(0)-\Phi_{\sigma}\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+\sigma^{2}\log(T)\right].

(2.64)

3
Random evolution families and the forward integral

In this preparatory section, we consider a type of random evolution family associated to
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
that we will encounter throughout this paper. In particular,
we consider the spatial domain
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
for some fixed dimension
d\geq 1

and introduce the family of random linear operators
\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t):\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(H^{2},L^{2})

that act as

[\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t)(\omega)u](x,y)=\mathcal{[}\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}u(\cdot,y)](x)+\nu(t,\omega)[\Delta_{y}u(x,\cdot)](y),

(3.1)

for
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
y\in\mathbb{T}^{d-1},

0\leq t\leq T,
and
\omega\in\Omega
. We impose the following conditions on the coefficient function
\nu
and the general setting that we consider in this section.

(H
\nu
)

The function
\nu:[0,T]\times\Omega\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is progressively measurable and continuous with respect to the time variable
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely. In addition, there exist two positive constants
k_{\nu},K_{\nu}>0
such that
k_{\nu}\leq\nu(t)\leq K_{\nu}
holds for all
t\in[0,T]
.

(HE)

Either (Hf-Lip) is satisfied with
k\geq 0
or we have
k=1
with (Hf-Cub). Furthermore,
(HTw), (HS) and (H
\nu
) all hold and we have
T\geq 1
.

It is common practice to suppress the dependency of
\omega\in\Omega
, and we will do that too unless we want to be explicit. Since both
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
and
\Delta_{y}
can be seen as unbounded operators on
L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
that act pointwise on the ‘other’ coordinate, we will typically write (
3.1
) in the shorter form

\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t)=\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}+\nu(t)\Delta_{y}.

(3.2)

At times we will restrict the operators
\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t)
to spaces of smoother functions while using the same notation, writing
\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t):\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(H^{k+2},H^{k})
, for example. Observe that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. the operators

\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t)
are sectorial for all
0\leq t\leq T
, as they are lower order perturbations to the diffusive operators
\partial_{x}^{2}+\nu(t)\Delta_{y}

[
61
, Prop. 3.2.2(iii)]
.

In §
3.1
we show that any family of random linear operators defined in (
3.1
) satisfying (H
\nu
) generates an adapted
C_{0}
-evolution family with convenient analytic properties. For these
\omega
-dependent evolution families, we introduce in §
3.2
a non-standard type of stochastic integration—referred to as forward integrals—and provide an essential maximal inequality. We proceed in §
3.3
by exploiting the decaying part of the evolution to formulate a maximal inequality in
H^{k}
with respect to a weight that decays exponentially in time. This allows us in §
3.4
to formulate a maximal regularity result that achieves control over the integrated
H^{k+1}
-norm. Ultimately, in §
3.5
we study the time-dependence of our bounds and provide conditions that gaurantee a logarithmic growth rate for stochastic convolutions with our evolution family.

3.1
Basic properties

In order to understand the random flow
E(t,s)
generated by the
family of random linear operators in (
3.1
),
we consider the linear initial value problem

\partial_{t}v=\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t)v,\quad v(s)=v_{s}.

(3.3)

Writing
\hat{v}
to denote the Fourier transform with respect to the transverse direction, i.e.,

\hat{v}(x,\xi)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}\langle y,\xi\rangle}v(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1},

(3.4)

the initial value problem (
3.3
) transforms into

\partial_{t}\hat{v}=(\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}-\lambda_{1}\nu(t)|\xi|^{2})\hat{v},\quad\hat{v}(s)=\hat{v}_{s},

(3.5)

where
\lambda_{1}=4\pi^{2}/|\mathbb{T}|^{2}
denotes the first non-zero eigenvalue of the Laplacian
\Delta_{y}
.
An explicit solution of the equation above in terms of the analytic
C_{0}
-semigroup
S_{\rm tw}(t)

generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}

is given by

\hat{v}(t)=S_{\rm tw}(t)e^{-\lambda_{1}|\xi|^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r}\hat{v}_{s}.

(3.6)

Applying the inversion formula

v(x,y)=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}\langle y,\xi\rangle}\hat{v}(x,\xi),

(3.7)

we hence obtain the semi-explicit expression

[E(t,s)v](x,y)=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}\langle y,\xi\rangle}S_{\rm tw}(t-s)e^{-\lambda_{1}|\xi|^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r}\hat{v}(x,\xi)

(3.8)

for the evolution family associated to (
3.3
).
Note that (
3.8
) reduces to the expression found in
[
39
]
when considering the autonomous case
\nu\equiv 1
.

Our first result establishes several useful properties
of this family
E(t,s)
, which is defined on the set

{\triangle}=\{(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}:s\leq t\}
.
In summary,
E(t,s)

is an adapted
C_{0}
-evolution family in
H^{k}=H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
that inherits several important features from the analytic semigroup
S_{\rm tw}(t)
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose that
(HE)
holds.
Then the random family
(E(t,s,\omega))_{(s,t)\in\triangle,\omega\in\Omega}
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

There exists a constant
M>0
that does not depend on
T
so that
\|E(t,s)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}\leq M
holds for all
(s,t)\in\triangle
;

(ii)

E(s,s)=I
for all
s\in[0,T]
;

(iii)

E(t,s)=E(t,r)E(r,s)
for all
0\leq s\leq r\leq t\leq T
;

(iv)

The mapping
\triangle\ni(t,s)\mapsto E(t,s)
is strongly continuous, i.e.,

(t,s)\mapsto E(t,s)v
is continuous for all
v\in H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
;

(v)

E(t,s)v
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for all
(s,t)\in\triangle
and
v\in H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
;

(vi)

For every
s<t,
one has
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}E(t,s)=\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t)E(t,s)
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}E(t,s)=-E(t,s)\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(s)
, and there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
\omega\in\Omega
, such that

\|\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t)E(t,s)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}\leq C(t-s)^{-1}.

(3.9)

Proof.

Observe first that for
k>0
, we have

\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}E(t,s)v(x,y)=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}\langle y,\xi\rangle}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}S_{\rm tw}(t-s)e^{-\lambda_{1}|\xi|^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r}\widehat{\partial_{y}^{\beta}v}(x,\xi),

(3.10)

where
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d-1}
with
\alpha+|\beta|\leq k.
Applying Plancherel’s theorem twice yields

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\partial_{y}^{\beta}E(t,s)v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle=\|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}E(t,s)\partial_{y}^{\beta}v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}

(3.11)

\displaystyle\leq M\|\partial_{y}^{\beta}v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}

\displaystyle\leq M\|v\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

where we choose a sufficiently large
M\geq 1
for which
\|S_{\rm tw}(t)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\leq M
holds for all
\alpha\leq k
. This yields (i).
Properties (ii), (iii) and (v) are immediate from the definition (
3.8
).

Turning to (iv), the uniform bound in (i) implies that it suffices to establish the continuity for
v
in a dense set
W\subset H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
; see, e.g., the proof of
[
15
, Prop I.5.3]
. Without loss, we restrict ourselves to
n=1
and choose
W
to be the set
of product functions
w(x,y)=w_{1}(x)w_{2}(y)
. Here

w_{1}(x)
is any element in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
and
w_{2}(y)
is either the real or imaginary part of

e^{\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}\langle y,\zeta\rangle},\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}.

(3.12)

Exploiting Plancherel’s theorem once more, together with the identity (
3.10
), we find

\displaystyle\|E(\tau,\sigma)w-E(t,s)w\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=\|S_{\rm tw}(\tau-\sigma)w_{1}h(\tau,\sigma;\zeta)-S_{\rm tw}(t-s)w_{1}h(t,s;\zeta)\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}

(3.13)

\displaystyle\leq\|S_{\rm tw}(\tau-\sigma)w_{1}-S_{\rm tw}(t-s)w_{1}\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle+M\|w_{1}\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}|h(\tau,\sigma;\zeta)-h(t,s;\zeta)|,

where
h(t,s;\zeta)=e^{-\lambda_{1}|\zeta|^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r}
, which satisfies
|h(t,s;\zeta)|\leq 1.
Since
S_{\rm tw}(t)
is strongly continuous in
H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}),
it remains to show that
\Delta\to\mathbb{R},(t,s)\mapsto h(t,s;\zeta)
is continuous for every
\zeta\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
fixed. Since
|1-e^{ax}|\leq 2e^{a}|x|
for
x\leq 1
, we obtain

\displaystyle|h(\tau,\sigma;\zeta)-h(t,s;\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leq\big|1-e^{\lambda_{1}|\zeta|^{2}\int_{\sigma}^{\tau}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r-\lambda_{1}|\zeta|^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r}\big|

(3.14)

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}|\zeta|^{2}\left|{\int_{\sigma}^{\tau}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r-\int_{s}^{t}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}|\zeta|^{2}K_{\nu}\big[|\tau-t|+|\sigma-s|\big]<\varepsilon,

assuming
|t-\tau|,|s-\sigma|<\varepsilon/4\lambda_{1}|\zeta|^{2}K_{\nu}
, where
\varepsilon\ll 1
needs to be sufficiently small.

Finally, property (vi) follows from the fact that we can swap differentiation and summation in this situation, that
S_{\rm tw}(t)
satisfies the properties

\|\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}S_{\rm tw}(t-s)v\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}S_{\rm tw}(t-s)v\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq(t-s)^{-1}\|v\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(3.15)

and that the
elementary estimate
ue^{-2u}\leq 1
for
u\geq 0
allows us to deduce the inequality

\lambda_{1}|\xi|^{2}e^{-2\lambda_{1}|\xi|^{2}k_{\nu}t}\leq t/k_{\nu},\quad t\geq 0,

(3.16)

on account of (H
\nu
).
∎

Decompositions of
E(t,s)
.

In view of the semi-explicit expression (
3.8
)
for the random evolution family
E(t,s)
, it is convenient to introduce the bounded linear operators
F(t,s,\omega):H^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to H^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
that act as

F(t,s)v(y)=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}y\cdot\xi}e^{-\lambda_{1}|\xi|^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r}\hat{v}(\xi),

(3.17)

for any
(s,t)\in\triangle
and
\omega\in\Omega
. Recall,
\hat{v}
now denotes the usual Fourier transform on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};\mathbb{R}^{n}).

This allows us to obtain the commuting decomposition

E(t,s)=F(t,s)S_{\rm tw}(t-s)=S_{\rm tw}(t-s)F(t,s)

(3.18)

for any
(s,t)\in\triangle
.
Here
S_{\rm tw}(t)
and
F(t,s)
can be interpreted
as a semigroup and evolution family, respectively,
on the full space
H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})

that act pointwise on the “other” coordinate, i.e.,

[S_{\rm tw}(t)v](x,y)=[S_{\rm tw}(t)v(\cdot,y)](x),\quad[F(t,s)v](x,y)=[F(t,s)v(x,\cdot)](y).

(3.19)

Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\|S_{\rm tw}(t)v\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|S_{\rm tw}(t)v\|_{H^{\ell}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};H^{k-\ell}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}

(3.20)

\displaystyle\leq CM\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|v\|_{H^{\ell}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};H^{k-\ell}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}

\displaystyle\leq CMc^{-1}\|v\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

for some constants
C,c>0
.
In fact,
F(t,s)
represents a
C_{0}
-family of contractions, since

\|F(t,s)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}=\|F(t,s)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\leq 1.

(3.21)

However in general
E(t,s)
is not a family of contractions, since
S_{\rm tw}(t)
is not. Note that for
\nu\equiv 1
we simply have
F(t,s)=e^{(t-s)\Delta_{y}}
.

Importantly, note that we can actually decompose
E
even further by writing

E(t,s,\omega)=H(t,s,\omega)G(t-s)S_{\rm tw}(t-s),

(3.22)

using the contractive random evolution family

H(t,s,\omega)v(y)=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}y\cdot\xi}e^{-\lambda_{1}|\xi|^{2}\int_{s}^{t}[\nu(r)-\frac{1}{2}k_{\nu}]\mathrm{d}r}\hat{v}(\xi)

(3.23)

and the contractive analytic semigroup

G(t)=e^{\frac{1}{2}k_{\nu}t\Delta_{y}}.

(3.24)

The key benefit of this decomposition is that

E_{\rm tw}(t)=G(t)S_{\rm tw}(t)

(3.25)

defines a (deterministic) analytic semigroup on
H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
that hence also has smoothening properties in the
y
-direction; see, e.g., (
3.138
) and (
3.149
).

Spectral projections.

Upon introducing the (one-dimensional) spectral projection

P_{\rm tw}u=\langle u,\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\Phi_{0}^{\prime}

(3.26)

together with its complement
P^{\perp}_{\rm tw}=I-P_{\rm tw}
,
we note that the spectral gap assumption (HS)
implies the exponential decay

\|S_{\rm tw}(t)P^{\perp}_{\rm tw}u\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq Me^{-\beta t}\|P^{\perp}_{\rm tw}u\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})};

(3.27)

see for example
[
61
]
. On the other hand,
after introducing the averaging operator

P_{\rm avg}u=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}u(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

(3.28)

with respect to the
y
-direction
and writing
P_{\rm avg}^{\perp}=I-P_{\rm avg}
,
it is clear
from (
3.17
) that

\|F(t,s)P_{\rm avg}^{\perp}v\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq e^{-\lambda_{1}\kappa_{\nu}(t-s)}\|P_{\rm avg}^{\perp}v\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(3.29)

since the
\xi=0
component of the Fourier transform is averaged out and hence vanishes.

We extend these projections (which are bounded on
H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
H^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, respectively) to bounded operators on the full function space
H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
by writing

[P_{\rm tw}u](x,y)=\langle u(\cdot,y),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(x)\quad\text{and}\quad[P_{\rm avg}u](x,y)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}u(x,y^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}.

(3.30)

Observe that the bounds in (
3.27
)
and (
3.29
) carry over to the full spatial norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
by estimates
such as (
3.20
)
and direct inspection of the Fourier
representation (
3.17
).

An important role in this paper is reserved for the
bounded linear operator
P
on
H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})

that acts as

P=P_{\rm tw}P_{\rm avg}=P_{\rm avg}P_{\rm tw},

(3.31)

implying that it is also a projection. Observe that
Pv=0
holds if and only if
\langle v,\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0.
Further, introducing the complement

P^{\perp}=I-P=P^{\perp}_{\rm tw}+P_{\rm tw}P^{\perp}_{\rm avg},

(3.32)

we see that
E(t,s)P^{\perp}
admits exponential decay.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose that
(HE)
holds.
Then there exists a constant
M\geq 1
for which we have

\|E(t,s)P^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k},H^{k})}\leq Me^{-\mu(t-s)},

(3.33)

together with

\|E(t,s)P^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k},H^{k+1})}\leq M\max((t-s)^{-1/2},1)e^{-\mu(t-s)},

(3.34)

where
\mu=\min\{\beta,\lambda_{1}k_{\nu}\}>0.

Proof.

The bound (
3.33
) follows directly from the representation
(
3.32
) together with the estimates

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|E(t,s)P^{\perp}_{\rm tw}v\|_{H^{k}}&\leq&\|F(t,s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{k})}\|S(t-s)P^{\perp}_{\rm tw}v\|_{H^{k}}\\[5.69046pt]
&\leq&Me^{-\beta(t-s)}\|P^{\perp}_{\rm tw}v\|_{H^{k}}\end{array}

(3.35)

and

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|E(t,s)P_{\rm tw}P^{\perp}_{\rm avg}v\|_{H^{k}}&\leq&\|S(t-s)P_{\rm tw}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}\|F(t,s)P^{\perp}_{\rm avg}v\|_{H^{k}}\\[5.69046pt]
&\leq&Me^{-\lambda_{1}k_{\nu}(t-s)}\|P^{\perp}_{\rm avg}v\|_{H^{k}},\end{array}

(3.36)

increasing
M
if necessary. On the other hand,
(
3.34
) follows from the standard semigroup bound

\|S_{\rm tw}(t)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}),H^{k+1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\leq M\max\{t^{-1/2},1\}

(3.37)

and inspection of the identity (
3.10
).
∎

Remark 3.3
.
