Note that we do not assume any Hölder continuity on our coefficient
\nu
, as opposed to the setting of

[
15
, Ch. VI.9]
and
[
1
,
2
,
74
,
98
]
. Assuming more regularity would allow us to write the evolution family as

E(t,s)=e^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(s)}+\int_{s}^{t}Z(t,r)\,\mathrm{d}r,

(3.38)

where
Z(t,r)
, see
[
1
, eq. (2.7)]
for instance, is expressed in terms of inductively defined operators that are related to the resolvent of
\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(s)
.

3.2
Forward integrability and maximal inequalities

We will now set out to construct a suitable notion for stochastic convolutions against the random evolution family
E(t,s)
. The key issue that needs to be addressed is that
E(t,s)
is only measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t}
and not
\mathcal{F}_{s}
, precluding the use of the regular Itô integral. In addition,
E(t,s)
is not a family of contractions, which prevents us from appealing directly to existing results.

As customary, we let
\mathcal{W}
be a real separable Hilbert space with orthonormal basis
(e_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and assume
Q\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{W})
to be a non-negative symmetric operator. We can then consider the Hilbert space
\mathcal{W}_{Q}=Q^{1/2}(\mathcal{W})
endowed with its natural inner product

\langle v,w\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{Q}}=\langle Q^{-1/2}v,Q^{-1/2}w\rangle_{\mathcal{W}},

(3.39)

which has
(\sqrt{Q}e_{k})_{k\geq 0}
as an orthonormal basis.
7
7

7

We tacitly neglect any possible zero element of the set
(\sqrt{Q}e_{k})_{k\geq 0}
caused by the fact that
Q
is only a non-negative and not a positive operator.

Following
[
11
,
21
,
24
,
30
,
42
,
73
]
we consider a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})

[
76
]

and set to construct a cylindrical
Q
-Wiener process
W^{Q}=(W^{Q}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
that is adapted to the filtration
\mathbb{F}
. We consider a set
(\beta_{k})_{k\geq 0}
of independent standard Brownian motions adapted to
\mathbb{F}
and write

W_{t}^{Q}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{Q}e_{k}\beta_{k}(t),

(3.40)

which converges in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{W}_{\rm ext})
for some larger (abstract) space
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{W}_{\rm ext}
that is guaranteed to exist by the discussion in
[
24
]
;
see also
[
30
, Sec. 5.1]
for additional background information.

For any Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and
p\geq 2
,
we introduce the class of processes

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{F};HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};\mathcal{H}))

\displaystyle=\{B\in L^{p}(\Omega;L^{2}([0,T];HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};\mathcal{H}))):

(3.41)

\displaystyle B\text{ has a progressively measurable version}\},

for which Itô stochastic integrals with respect to
W^{Q}
can be defined. In fact,
we have the identity

\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}B(s)[\sqrt{Q}e_{k}]\mathrm{d}\beta_{k}(s),

(3.42)

where the convergence is in
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
and hence also in probability
[
42
]
. We remark that for any measurable and adapted process, we can find a progressive measurable version with an indistinguishable stochastic integral
[
65
, p. 68]
.

Setting out to drop the requirement concerning progressive measurability, we follow
[
55
]

and introduce the notion of a forward integral
with respect to the cylindrical
Q
-Wiener process
W^{Q}
.
We restrict ourselves to the Hilbert space setting, referring the reader to
[
70
]
for the analogous definitions for Banach spaces.

Definition 3.4
.

Suppose
G:[0,T]\times\Omega\to HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};\mathcal{H})
is an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable process such that for each
\xi\in\mathcal{W}
we
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. have
G[\xi]\in L^{1}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
. For any integer
n\geq 1
we define

I^{-}(G,n)=n\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}G(s)[\sqrt{Q}{e_{k}}](\beta_{k}(s+1/n)-\beta_{k}(s))\mathrm{d}s.

(3.43)

If the sequence
\left(I^{-}(G,n)\right)_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges in probability, then
G
is said to be forward integrable (with respect to
\mathcal{H}
), and we denote the limiting process
\lim_{n\to\infty}I^{-}(G,n)
either by
I^{-}(G)
or

\int_{0}^{T}G(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}.

(3.44)

In the special case
G\in\mathcal{N}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{F};HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};\mathcal{H}))
,
the infinite sequence
(I^{-}(G,n))_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges in probability and the limit coincides with the Itô-integral
[
75
, Prop. 3.2]
.
In other words, the forward integral is indeed a proper extension of the Itô-integral.
One of the advantages over other extensions of the Itô-integral, such as the Skorohod integral, is that one may simply pull any random operator
A:\Omega\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
out of the integral. That is, when
G
is forward integrable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
, then
AG
is forward integrable with respect to
\mathcal{K}
, and

\int_{0}^{t}AG(s)\mathrm{d}W^{-}_{s}=A\int_{0}^{t}G(s)\mathrm{d}W^{-}_{s},

(3.45)

for all
0\leq t\leq T.

Turning towards stochastic convolutions with
E(t,s)
in
\mathcal{H}=H^{k}=H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
we now follow
[
70
]
by first considering adapted finite-rank step processes
B

that map into
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+2})
, providing extra
smoothness as compared to the target space
H^{k}
in which we want to understand the convolution.
These processes can be written as

B(t)[\sqrt{Q}e_{\ell}]=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{1}_{\left(t_{i-1},t_{i}\right]}(t)\mathbf{1}_{A_{ij}}{h_{ij\ell}},

(3.46)

where
A_{ij}\in\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}
and
h_{ij\ell}\in H^{k+2}
, for any
1\leq i\leq I
,
1\leq j\leq J
and
1\leq\ell\leq L
, for some
I,J,L<\infty
. For the remaining
\ell>L
we set
B(s)[\sqrt{Q}e_{\ell}]=0.

Proposition 3.5
.

Suppose that
(HE)
holds.
Consider
any adapted finite-rank step process
B
that takes values in
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+2})
. Then the stochastic process
(E(t,s)B(s))_{s\in[0,t]}
is forward integrable with respect to
H^{k}
on the interval
[0,t]
, for any
0\leq t\leq T,
and
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. we have

\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}=E(t,0)\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q}+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}E(t,s)\int_{s}^{t}B(r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.47)

Moreover, the process
\left(\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right)_{t\in[0,T]}
has a continuous version in
H^{k}
.

Proof.

Since the process
B
takes values in the the domain of
\mathcal{L}_{\nu}
, item (vi) of Proposition
3.1
leads to the bound

\|E(t,s,\omega)v\|_{W^{1,1}([0,t];H^{k})}\leq C\|v\|_{H^{k+2}}

(3.48)

for some constant
C>0
, independent of
\omega\in\Omega
and
(s,t)\in\triangle
.
In particular, the right-hand side of (
3.47
) is well-defined
and can indeed be identified as the forward integral of
E(t,\cdot)B(\cdot)
by applying
[
75
, Cor. 5.3]
. The pathwise continuity of the Itô integral
\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q}
in
H^{k+2}

directly implies that the forward integral
(
3.47
)
admits a continuous version in
H^{k}
.
∎

Combining (
3.45
) and Proposition
3.5
, we see that for any

B
that satisfies the conditions of the latter we may split stochastic convolutions, in the sense that
for any
0\leq\sigma\leq\tau\leq t
we may write

\displaystyle\int_{\sigma}^{\tau}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}-\int_{0}^{\sigma}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}

(3.49)

\displaystyle=E(t,\tau)\int_{0}^{\tau}E(\tau,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}-E(t,\sigma)\int_{0}^{\sigma}E(\sigma,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}.

More precisely, the integrals on the right hand side are well-defined (when assuming the conditions in Proposition
3.5
) and can hence be seen as a definition for the left hand side of (
3.49
).

A major step towards interpreting stochastic convolutions of
E(t,s)
with general processes
B
is the derivation
of some maximal inequality, which
provides an estimate for (
3.47
) that
does not rely on the additional
H^{k+2}
-smoothness.
We refer to
[
69
,
99
]

for discussions on maximal inequalities for ordinary Itô convolutions, which typically rely on factorisation or dilation arguments that require the semigroups to be contractive or to admit an
H^{\infty}
-calculus.
In our case, we
exploit the decomposition (
3.18
) and utilise the fact that
S_{\rm tw}(t)
admits an
H^{\infty}
-calculus after projecting out the neutral mode. Note that the factorisation method, although applicable in a much wider setting, leads to a bound that is less sharp (see also the discussion after Theorem
3.7
).

In particular, we proceed by splitting the process
B
as

B(s)=P_{\rm tw}B(s)+P_{\rm tw}^{\perp}B(s),

(3.50)

noting that the spectral projection acts as

P_{\rm tw}B(s)[w]=\langle B(s)[w],\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\Phi_{0}^{\prime}

(3.51)

for all
w\in\mathcal{W}_{Q}
.
Since
S_{\rm tw}(t)\Phi_{0}^{\prime}=\Phi_{0}^{\prime}
holds, we have

E(t,s)P_{\rm tw}B(s)=F(t,s)P_{\rm tw}B(s),

(3.52)

allowing us to exploit the fact that
F
is a contractive (random) evolution family.

Turning to the complementary process
P_{\rm tw}^{\perp}B
,
we shall make us of the subspaces

H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})=\{v\in H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}):P_{\rm tw}v=0\},

(3.53)

for any
k\geq 0
, which are again Hilbert spaces when endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}}
.
Considering the restricted operator

\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}:H^{k+2}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
we note that the perturbation arguments in

[
101
, Sec. 8]
can be combined with the fact that the restriction of
-\partial_{x}^{2}

to
H^{k+2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
admits a bounded
H^{\infty}
-calculus
[
32
, Prop 10.2.23]
,
to conclude that
-\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
is sectorial in
H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and admits a bounded
H^{\infty}
-calculus of angle strictly smaller then
\pi/2
. The details can be found in
[
27
, Lem. 2.9.7]
where the
k=0
case was considered, relying crucially on the fact that the simple translational eigenvalue at zero has been removed from the spectrum of
-\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}
.

In view of
[
99
, Prop. 3.1]
, there exists
8
8

8

With regards to the notation used in
[
37
,
71
,
99
]
,
we point out that
\gamma(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, since
H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a Hilbert space, which is of type 2 as well as cotype 2
[
37
, Rem. 4.7]
.

an equivalent norm
\vvvert\cdot\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

on
H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which is given by

\vvvert v\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\Bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{1/2}S_{\rm tw}(r)v\|^{2}_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}r\Bigg]^{1/2},

(3.54)

ensuring that
S_{\rm tw}(t)
restricted to
H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})

is contractive with respect to this norm.
Turning to the full spatial domain, we now introduce the notation

H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})=\{v\in H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}):P_{\rm tw}v=0\}

(3.55)

and provide it with the norm

\vvvert v\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\left[\sum_{\beta:|\beta|\leq k}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\vvvert\partial_{y}^{\beta}v(\cdot,y)\vvvert_{H^{k-|\beta|}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\,dy\right]^{1/2},

(3.56)

where the sum is with respect to multi-indices
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}_{\geq 0}
. This norm is again equivalent to the usual
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}}
norm, which means that there exist constants
C,c>0
for which

c\|v\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\vvvert v\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C\|v\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(3.57)

for all
v\in H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The full evolution family
E(t,s)
can be restricted to this subspace, where it is contractive.

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose that
(HE)
holds.
Then for any
(s,t)\in\triangle
and any
v\in H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have
E(t,s)v\in H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
together with the bound

\vvvert E(t,s)v\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\vvvert v\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(3.58)

Proof.

Let us write
H^{k}_{\perp}=H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}).
The invariance of
H^{k}_{\perp}
under the evolution
E(t,s)

follows from the fact that inner products with respect to
x
commute with the Fourier transform with respect to
y
.
As in the proof of Proposition
3.1
,
the operator
\partial_{y}^{\beta}
commutes
with both
F(t,s)
and
S_{\rm tw}(t)
.
Hence,
for any
v\in H^{k}_{\perp}

we compute

\displaystyle\vvvert E(t,s)v\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta:|\beta|\leq k}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\vvvert[\partial_{y}^{\beta}F(t,s)S_{\rm tw}(t-s)v](\cdot,y)\vvvert_{H^{k-|\beta|}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}y

(3.59)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta:|\beta|\leq k}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{1/2}S_{\rm tw}(r)[\partial_{y}^{\beta}F(t,s)S_{\rm tw}(t-s)v](\cdot,y)\|^{2}_{H^{k-|\beta|}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta:|\beta|\leq k}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\|[F(t,s)\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{1/2}S_{\rm tw}(r)S_{\rm tw}(t-s)\partial_{y}^{\beta}v](\cdot,y)\|^{2}_{H^{k-|\beta|}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta:|\beta|\leq k}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\|[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{1/2}S_{\rm tw}(r+t-s)\partial_{y}^{\beta}v](\cdot,y)\|^{2}_{H^{k-|\beta|}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}r,

where we used the fact that
F(t,s)
is a contraction
which, in addition, acts pointwise with respect to
x
.
By performing the substitution
r+t-s\mapsto r
, we find

\displaystyle\vvvert E(t,s)v\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta:|\beta|\leq k}\int_{t-s}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\|[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{1/2}S_{\rm tw}(r)\partial_{y}^{\beta}v](\cdot,y)\|^{2}_{H^{k-|\beta|}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}r

(3.60)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta:|\beta|\leq k}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\|[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{1/2}S_{\rm tw}(r)\partial_{y}^{\beta}v](\cdot,y)\|^{2}_{H^{k-|\beta|}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta:|\beta|\leq k}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{1/2}S_{\rm tw}(r)\partial_{y}^{\beta}v](\cdot,y)\|^{2}_{H^{k-|\beta|}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta:|\beta|\leq k}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\vvvert[\partial_{y}^{\beta}v](\cdot,y)\vvvert^{2}_{H^{k-|\beta|}_{\perp}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\vvvert v\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}}^{2},

as desired.
∎

We are now ready to state our main result here
and provide a maximal inequality for forward integrals. It can be seen as an extension of
[
70
, Thm. 6.4]
, in the sense that
E(t,s)
itself is not a family of contractions on the full space
H^{k}=H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. It is worth noting that for
p=1

one can use an alternative direct approach utilising the Fourier transform.

Theorem 3.7

(maximal inequality)
.

Suppose that
(HE)
holds.
Then there exists a constant
K_{\rm cnv}>0
that does not depend on
T

so that for any adapted finite-rank step process
B

that takes values in
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+2})
,
and for every
p\geq 1
, we have the bound

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W^{-}_{s}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq p^{p}K_{\rm cnv}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}.

(3.61)

Proof.

In light of the decomposition (
3.50
),
we write

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{I}&=&\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)P_{\rm tw}B(s)\mathrm{d}W^{-}_{s}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p},\\[5.69046pt]
\mathcal{I}_{\perp}&=&\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)P_{\rm tw}^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W^{-}_{s}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p},\end{array}

(3.62)

and note that

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W^{-}_{s}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq 2^{2p-1}\left(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{I}_{\perp}\right).

(3.63)

In view of (
3.52
) and the contractivity of
F(t,s)
, we may apply

[
70
, Thm. 6.4]
to find

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}F(t,s)P_{\rm tw}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

(3.64)

\displaystyle\leq p^{p}K_{F}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\|P_{\rm tw}B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}

\displaystyle\leq p^{p}K_{F}^{2p}\|P_{\rm tw}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}

for some constant
K_{F}
. In a similar fashion,
we may exploit Lemma
3.6

and the equivalence (
3.57
)
to compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\perp}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2p}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\vvvert\int_{0}^{T}E(t,s){P_{\rm tw}^{\perp}B(s)}\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\vvvert_{H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2p}

(3.65)

\displaystyle\leq p^{p}C^{2p}K_{E}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\vvvert P_{\rm tw}^{\perp}B(s)\vvvert^{2}_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k}_{\perp}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}

\displaystyle\leq p^{p}C^{2p}K_{E}^{2p}c^{-2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\|P_{\rm tw}^{\perp}B(s)\|^{2}_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}

\displaystyle\leq p^{p}C^{2p}K_{E}^{2p}c^{-2p}\|P_{\rm tw}^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\|B(s)\|^{2}_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}

for some constant
K_{E}
. By setting
K_{\rm cnv}=2\max\{K_{F}\|P_{\rm tw}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))},CK_{E}c^{-1}\|P_{\rm tw}^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\},

the desired bound follows.
∎

In retrospect, the representation (
3.47
) allows us (for
T\geq 1
) to derive the (crude) pathwise bound

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}\leq KT\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q}\right\|_{H^{k+2}},

(3.66)

for some
K>0.
Taking
L^{2p}(\Omega)
-norms on both sides, and
appealing to
[
70
, Prop. 2.1 and Rem. 2.2]
, results into a similar inequality as in (
3.61
),
but then with
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+2})
instead of
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})
and an additional factor of
T^{2p}
on the right hand side, which would both be detrimental to our future estimates.
Note that the factorisation method does not lead to a loss of regularity, but the maximal inequality that one obtains is less sharp. Indeed, the constant
K_{\rm cnv}
would depend on time, the power
p
on the right hand side of (
3.61
) would be inside of the integral, and the result would only hold for
p>1.

Since the embeddings
H^{k+2}\hookrightarrow H^{k}
are dense, for any
k\geq 0,
we can use the maximal inequality above to extend the definition of the forward integral for progressively measurable processes that attain values in
H^{k}
. This is achieved in a standard fashion by approximating these processes by adapted step processes of finite-rank with values in
H^{k+2}
.

Corollary 3.8

(unique extension)
.

Suppose that
(HE)
holds.
Then for all
p\geq 2
,
the mapping

B\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}

(3.67)

has a unique extension to a continuous linear operator

J_{p}:\mathcal{N}^{p}([0,t];\mathbb{F};HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k}))\rightarrow L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,t];H^{k})),

(3.68)

for any
0\leq t\leq T.

Remark 3.9
.

We continue to denote the generalised forward integral
J_{p}B
as

\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-},

and emphasise the fact that the maximal inequality in (
3.61
)
remains valid. However, for general
B\in\mathcal{N}^{p}
,
it is not yet known whether the stochastic process
J_{p}B
is given by a forward stochastic convolution again, i.e., whether
I^{-}\big(E(t,\cdot)B(\cdot)\big)
exists in the sense of Definition
3.4
. On the other hand, if the latter does exist, then it is necessarily equal to
J_{p}B
.

3.3
Weighted decay

Although of fundamental importance, the maximal inequality
(
3.61
) in its current form does not allow us to exploit the decay of the evolution family
E(t,s)P^{\perp}
. Let us introduce the shorthand notation

\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)=\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)P^{\perp}B(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}.

(3.69)

A first step is taken here by considering the process
B
in a space that is exponentially weighted with respect to time. In this case one can also include the weight in the supremum bound.

Proposition 3.10

(weighted decay estimate)
.

Suppose that
(HE)
holds
and pick
\varepsilon\in(0,2\mu)
.
Then there exists a constant
K_{\rm dc}>0
that does not depend on
T

so that for any process

B\in\mathcal{N}^{2p}\big([0,T];\mathbb{F};HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k}\big),

(3.70)

and every
p\geq 1
, we have the bound

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|e^{-\varepsilon/2(T-t)}\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq p^{p}K_{\rm dc}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}.

(3.71)

In particular, for any
0\leq t\leq T
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq p^{p}K^{2p}_{\rm dc}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}.

(3.72)

The key ingredient is that representation (
3.47
) allows us to split up convolutions in such a way that the decay becomes visible.
In particular, we start again by considering an adapted finite-rank step process
B
that takes values in
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+2})
. This allows us
to consider a time
N\leq t\leq N+1
, for some integer
N
, and split up the convolution
as

\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)=I_{N;I}(t)+I_{N;II}(t),

(3.73)

where

\begin{array}[]{lcl}I_{N;I}(t)&=&\sum_{j=1}^{N}E(t,j)P^{\perp}\int_{0}^{j}E(j,s)B(s)\mathbf{1}_{j-1\leq s\leq j}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-},\\[5.69046pt]
I_{N;II}(t)&=&\int_{N}^{t}E(t,s)P^{\perp}B(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}.\end{array}

(3.74)

Note that, in the above, we have exploited
the commutation relation
P^{\perp}E(t,s)=E(t,s)P^{\perp}
.

Proof of Proposition
3.10
.

Without loss we assume
T\in\mathbb{N}
. We first
consider an adapted finite-rank step process
B

that takes values in
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+2})
.
In particular, the splitting (
3.73
) holds.
Using
the bound (
3.33
),
we hence obtain the initial estimate

\displaystyle\left\|I_{N;I}(t)\right\|_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}\|E(t,j)P^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}\left\|\int_{j-1}^{j}E(j,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}

(3.75)

\displaystyle\leq Me^{-\mu(t-N)}\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{-\mu(N-j)}\left\|\int_{j-1}^{j}E(j,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}.

An application of Hölder’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\left\|I_{N;I}(t)\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq M^{2p}e^{-2\mu p(t-N)}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{-\frac{(2\mu-\varepsilon)}{2}(N-j)}\cdot e^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}(N-j)}\left\|\int_{j-1}^{j}E(j,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}\right]^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq M^{2p}e^{-2\mu p(t-N)}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{-\frac{p(2\mu-\varepsilon)}{2p-1}(N-j)}\right]^{2p-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{-p\varepsilon(N-j)}\left\|\int_{j-1}^{j}E(j,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M^{2p}e^{-2\mu p(t-N)}}{\big(e^{\frac{p(2\mu-\varepsilon)}{2p-1}}-1\big)^{2p-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{-p\varepsilon(N-j)}\left\|\int_{j-1}^{j}E(j,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}.

(3.76)

Writing
C=Me^{1/(2(2\mu-\varepsilon)^{2})}
and observing

\frac{M^{2p}}{(e^{\frac{p(2\mu-\varepsilon)}{2p-1}}-1)^{2p-1}}\leq M^{2p}e^{p/(2\mu-\varepsilon)^{2}}=C^{2p},

(3.77)

we hence see that

\displaystyle\|e^{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon t}I_{N;I}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2p}e^{\varepsilon pN}\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{-p\varepsilon(N-j)}\,\left\|\int_{j-1}^{j}E(j,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

(3.78)

\displaystyle\leq C^{2p}\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{p\varepsilon j}\sup_{j^{\prime}\in[j-1,j]}\left\|\int_{j-1}^{j^{\prime}}E(j^{\prime},s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}.

In addition, we observe that

\displaystyle\|e^{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon t}I_{N;II}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\varepsilon pt}\left\|\int_{N}^{t}E(t,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

(3.79)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\varepsilon p(N+1)}\sup_{j^{\prime}\in[N,N+1]}\left\|\int_{N}^{j^{\prime}}E(j^{\prime},s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}.

Note that these estimates no longer explicitly depend on
t
.
As a consequence, this yields

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\big[e^{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon t}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)\|_{H^{k}}\big]^{2p}\leq 2^{2p}C^{2p}\sum_{j=1}^{T}e^{p\varepsilon j}\sup_{j^{\prime}\in[j-1,j]}\left\|\int_{j-1}^{j^{\prime}}E(j^{\prime},s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}.

(3.80)

Applying the maximal inequality (
3.61
), we may hence compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\big[e^{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon t}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)\|_{H^{k}}\big]^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p}C^{2p}K_{\rm cnv}^{2p}p^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{T}e^{p\varepsilon j}\ \mathbb{E}\left[\int_{j-1}^{j}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}

\displaystyle\leq p^{p}2^{2p}C^{2p}K_{\rm cnv}^{2p}e^{p\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\sum_{j=1}^{T}\left[\int_{j-1}^{j}e^{\varepsilon s}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}

(3.81)

\displaystyle\leq{p^{p}}[2CK_{\rm cnv}e^{\varepsilon/2}]^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}e^{\mu s}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p},

using the standard sequence space inequality
\|\cdot\|_{\ell^{p}}^{p}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\ell^{1}}^{p}
in the last step.
In a standard fashion, we now use Corollary
3.8
to extend the result to general processes
B
that satisfy (
3.70
).
∎

3.4
Maximal regularity

Our goal here is to examine the integrated
H^{k+1}
-norm
of stochastic convolutions with our evolution family
E(t,s)
. In particular, we consider the integral

\mathcal{I}^{\perp}_{B}=\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

(3.82)

with
\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B]
defined as in (
3.69
).
Our result here states that in a certain sense the

H^{k}
-supremum estimates from Theorem
3.7

and Proposition
3.10

can be combined to infer integrated control
over the
H^{k+1}
-norm of the convolution.

Proposition 3.11

(maximal regularity estimate)
.

Suppose that
(HE)
holds and pick
\varepsilon\in(0,2\mu).

Then there exists a constant
K_{\rm mr}>0
that does not depend on
T

so that for any

B\in\mathcal{N}^{2p}\big([0,T];\mathbb{F};HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k}\big),

(3.83)

and every integer
p\geq 1
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}^{\perp}_{B}]^{p}

\displaystyle\leq K_{\rm mr}^{p}\,\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

(3.84)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+p^{p/2}K_{\rm mr}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|B(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]^{p}.

Instead of appealing directly to a result for forward integrals, we will need to dive
deeper into the limiting process used in

[
70
]
to obtain maximal inequalities. This procedure crucially relies upon the introduction of a (small) delay
\delta>0
into the arguments of the random evolution family. In particular, we introduce the notation
x_{+}=\max\{0,x\}

and write

E^{\delta}(t,s)=E\big((t-\delta)_{+},(s-\delta)_{+}\big),\qquad\nu^{\delta}(t)=\nu((t-\delta)_{+}),

(3.85)

for
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
.

We proceed under the assumption that
B
is an
adapted finite-rank step process
that takes values in
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+3})
, instead of
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+2})
. Upon defining
the process

Z_{\delta}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}E^{\delta}(t,s)P^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-},

(3.86)

our main task is to establish bounds for the truncated integral

I_{\delta}=\int_{\delta}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\|Z_{\delta}(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(3.87)

which only involve
H^{k}
-norms. To see that this integral is well-defined, we follow step 3 in the proof of
[
70
, Thm. 6.4]
and remark that the arguments in the proof of Proposition
3.5
can be used to
obtain the
alternative representation

Z_{\delta}(t)=E((t-\delta)^{+},0)P^{\perp}\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q}+\int_{0}^{(t-\delta)^{+}}\partial_{s}E((t-\delta)^{+},s)P^{\perp}\int_{s+\delta}^{t}B(r)\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.88)

This implies that
Z_{\delta}
has continuous paths in
H^{k+1}
and that we have the pathwise bounds

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{\ell}}\leq KT\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}B(r)\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}\right\|_{H^{\ell+2}},

(3.89)

for any
0\leq\ell\leq k+1
, with
a constant
K>0
that is independent of
\delta
.

The key towards eliminating the dependence on the

H^{k+1}
-norms
lies within the introduction of the alternative equivalent inner products
9
9

9

In this definition and the subsequent identities (
3.91
) and (
3.93
) it is also allowed to take
\delta=0
.

\langle v,w\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}=\langle v,w\rangle_{H^{k}}+\langle v_{x},w_{x}\rangle_{H^{k}}+\nu^{\delta}(s)\langle\nabla_{y}v,\nabla_{y}w\rangle_{H^{k}},

(3.90)

for any
v,w\in H^{k+1}
and any
0\leq s\leq T
.
For now, we readily observe

\max\{1,k_{\nu}\}\langle v,v\rangle_{H^{k+1}}\leq\langle v,v\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\leq d\max\{1,K_{\nu}\}\langle v,v\rangle_{H^{k+1}},

(3.91)

which implies that

\displaystyle I_{\delta}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\nu}\int_{\delta}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\langle Z_{\delta}(s),Z_{\delta}(s)\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}s,

(3.92)

where
C_{\nu}=\min\{1,k_{\nu}^{-1}\}
. To appreciate the benefit of these inner products, we note that for
t\geq\delta

we may compute

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\langle E^{\delta}(t,s)v,E^{\delta}(t,s)w\rangle_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle=\langle[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}+\nu^{\delta}(t)\Delta_{y}]E^{\delta}(t,s)v,E^{\delta}(t,s)w\rangle_{H^{k}}

(3.93)

\displaystyle+\langle E(t,s)v,[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}+\nu^{\delta}(t)\Delta_{y}]E^{\delta}(t,s)w\rangle_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle=\langle E^{\delta}(t,s)v,[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}+\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj}-2\partial_{x}^{2}]E^{\delta}(t,s)w\rangle_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle+2\langle E^{\delta}(t,s)v,[\partial_{x}^{2}+\nu^{\delta}(t)\Delta_{y}]E^{\delta}(t,s)w\rangle_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle=\langle E^{\delta}(t,s)v,[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}+\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj}-2\partial_{x}^{2}]E^{\delta}(t,s)w\rangle_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle+2\langle E^{\delta}(t,s)v,E^{\delta}(t,s)w\rangle_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle-2\langle E^{\delta}(t,s)v,E^{\delta}(t,s)w\rangle_{t;\delta;k+1}.

In order to extract this derivative, we introduce
the bilinear form

\mathcal{J}^{\delta}(s_{2},s_{1})[v,w]=\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}e^{-\varepsilon(s_{2}-s)}\langle E^{\delta}(s,s_{1})v,E^{\delta}(s,s_{1})w\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}s,

(3.94)

for any
\delta\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq T
and any pair
v,w\in H^{k+1}
. Performing an integration by parts,
we obtain the decomposition

\mathcal{J}^{\delta}(s_{2},s_{1})[v,w]=\mathcal{J}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{expl}}(s_{2},s_{1})[v,w]+\mathcal{J}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{res}}(s_{2},s_{1})[v,w]

(3.95)

in terms of the explicit and residual bilinear forms

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{J}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{expl}}(s_{2},s_{1})[v,w]&=&-\frac{1}{2}\langle E^{\delta}(s_{2},s_{1})v,E^{\delta}(s_{2},s_{1})w\rangle_{H^{k}}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-\varepsilon(s_{2}-s_{1})}\langle v,w\rangle_{H^{k}},\\[5.69046pt]
\mathcal{J}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{res}}(s_{2},s_{1})[v,w]&=&\frac{1}{2}\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}e^{-\varepsilon(s_{2}-s)}\langle E^{\delta}(s,s_{1})v,A_{\varepsilon}E^{\delta}(s,s_{1})w\rangle_{H^{k}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\end{array}

(3.96)

where
A_{\varepsilon}=(2+\varepsilon)+\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}+\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj}-2\partial_{x}^{2}\in\mathscr{L}(H^{k}).

The main point is that these expressions can be bounded using
H^{k}
-norms only.

Lemma 3.12
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.11
.
Then there exists a constant
K>0
that does not depend on
T
so that for all sufficiently small
\delta\geq 0
we have the bounds

\begin{array}[]{lcl}|\mathcal{J}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{res}}(s_{2},s_{1})[v,w]|&\leq&K\|v\|_{H^{k}}\|w\|_{H^{k}}\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}e^{-\varepsilon(s_{2}-s)}\mathrm{d}s,\\[5.69046pt]
|\mathcal{J}^{\delta}(s_{2},s_{1})[v,w]|&\leq&K\|v\|_{H^{k}}\|w\|_{H^{k}},\end{array}

(3.97)

for any
\delta\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq T
and any
v,w\in H^{k+1}
.

Proof.

These bounds follow directly from inspection.
∎

Setting out to obtain a bound for the integral
I_{\delta}
for
\delta>0
fixed,
we choose an arbitrary partition

\pi=\{r_{0},r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}\}
, where
0=r_{0}<r_{1}<\ldots<r_{N}=T
and
|r_{j}-r_{j-1}|<\delta
for
j=1,\ldots,N
. We ensure that
\delta\in\pi
holds, i.e.,
r_{j_{\delta}-1}=\delta
for some integer
j_{\delta}
.
Furthermore, we introduce the shorthand notation
B^{j}(r)=B(r)\textbf{1}_{r_{j-1}\leq r\leq r_{j}}
together with the processes

Y_{\delta}^{j}(t)=\int_{r_{j-1}}^{t}E^{\delta}(t,r)B^{j}(r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q},

(3.98)

defined on
r_{j-1}\leq t\leq r_{j}
. On account of the delay and the mesh spacing constraint, we see that
E^{\delta}(t,r)v
is
\mathcal{F}_{r_{j-1}}
-measurable for all
v\in H^{k}
and

r_{j-1}\leq r\leq t\leq r_{j}
. In particular, the stochastic integrals in (
3.98
) are indeed well-defined in the Itô sense. In parallel to step 2 of the proof of
[
70
, Thm. 6.4]
, we note that we have the recursive relations

Z_{\delta}(t)=E^{\delta}(t,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})+Y_{\delta}^{j}(t),\qquad r_{j-1}\leq t\leq r_{j}.

(3.99)

Exploiting the bound (
3.92
) and breaking up the integral using the partition
\pi
leads
to the bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\delta}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\nu}\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})+Y_{\delta}^{j}(t),E^{\delta}(s,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})+Y_{\delta}^{j}(s)\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=C_{\nu}\big[\mathcal{H}_{I}+2\mathcal{H}_{II}+\mathcal{H}_{III}\big],

(3.100)

in which we have introduced the expressions

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),E^{\delta}(s,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}s,

(3.101)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{II}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),Y_{\delta}^{j}(s)\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}s,

(3.102)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{III}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\langle Y_{\delta}^{j}(s),Y_{\delta}^{j}(s)\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}s.

(3.103)

Recalling the definition (
3.94
)
and introducing the expressions

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{H}_{I;A}&=&\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\mathcal{J}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{res}}(r_{j},r_{j-1})[Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})],\\[5.69046pt]
\mathcal{H}_{I;B}&=&\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\mathcal{J}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{expl}}(r_{j},r_{j-1})[Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})],\end{array}

(3.104)

we see that
\mathcal{H}_{I}=\mathcal{H}_{I;A}+\mathcal{H}_{I;B}
.

We will treat the first term
\mathcal{H}_{I;A}
in a different—more direct—fashion than the others. This will be especially
convenient in §
3.5
, since the maximum of
this term over a set of different values for
T
reduces to the value for the largest
T
. Note that this term would disappear if one considers the simple grid
\pi=\{0,\delta,T\}
and sends
\delta\downarrow 0
, which would be possible when considering deterministic evolution families.
Intuitively,
this term collects the long-range cross talk between different elements of the set
\{B^{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
. In the regular setting these contributions are uncorrelated, but this is no longer the case here due to the probabilistic forward-looking nature of
\nu(t)
. More concretely, quadratic terms involving
Y_{\delta}
can be analysed with a mild Itô formula; this is not possible for quadratic terms involving
Z_{\delta}
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.11

together with the partition
\pi
.
Then there exists a constant
K>0
that does not depend on
T
so that we have the pathwise estimate

\textstyle\mathcal{H}_{I;A}\leq K\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}.

(3.105)

Proof.

Recalling Lemma
3.12
, a direct computation yields

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{H}_{I;A}&\leq&K\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\|Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})\|_{H^{k}}^{2}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(r_{j}-s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\\[5.69046pt]
&\leq&K\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\\[5.69046pt]
&=&K\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}\varepsilon^{-1}(1-e^{-\varepsilon(T-\delta)}),\end{array}

(3.106)

which provides the bound.
∎

After appropriate transformations, the remaining terms will all have a similar structure featuring various bilinear forms
\Gamma^{j}
on each mesh interval. All these satisfy the following structural constraint, allowing for a streamlined
estimation procedure.

(H
\Gamma
)

There exists
K_{\Gamma}>0
so that
for every
1\leq j\leq N
, any
r\in[r_{j-1},r_{j}]
,
and any
\omega\in\Omega
, the map
\Gamma^{j}(r,\omega):H^{k}\times H^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is a bilinear form that satisfies the bound

\Gamma^{j}(r)[v,w]\leq K_{\Gamma}\|v\|_{H^{k}}\|w\|_{H^{k}}.

(3.107)

In addition, the map
\omega\mapsto\Gamma^{j}(r,\omega)[v,w]
is
\mathcal{F}_{r_{j-1}}
-measurable for
any integer
1\leq j\leq N
, any
r\in[r_{j-1},r_{j}]
, and any
v,w\in H^{k}
.

Lemma 3.14
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.11

together with the partition
\pi
.
Then there exists a constant
K>0
that does not depend on
T
so that for any integer

p\geq 1
, any
\Gamma
satisfying
(H
\Gamma
)
,
and any progressively measurable
G\in L^{2p}(\Omega;C([0,T];H^{k}))
,
the integral expressions

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{I}_{s}[G,\Gamma]&=&\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\Gamma^{j}(r)\big[G(r),B^{j}(r)[\,\cdot\,]\big]\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q},\\[5.69046pt]
\mathcal{I}_{d}[G,\Gamma]&=&\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\Gamma^{j}(r)[B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k},B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k}]\,\mathrm{d}r\end{array}

(3.108)

satisfy the estimates

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\,\mathcal{I}_{s}[G,\Gamma]^{p}&\leq&\displaystyle p^{p/2}(KK_{\Gamma})^{p}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big[e^{-\varepsilon(T-t)}\|G(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}\Big]^{p}\\[5.69046pt]
&&\qquad\,\,\displaystyle+\,p^{p/2}(KK_{\Gamma})^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|B(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]^{p},\\[5.69046pt]
\mathcal{I}_{d}[G,\Gamma]&\leq&\displaystyle KK_{\Gamma}\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|B(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r.\end{array}

(3.109)

Proof.

Upon introducing the functions

\chi^{j}(r)=e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\mathbf{1}_{r_{j-1}\leq r\leq r_{j}}\Gamma^{j}(r)[G(r),B^{j}(r)]

(3.110)

and writing
\chi(r)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\chi^{j}(r)
,
we see that

\mathcal{I}_{s}[G,\Gamma]=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\chi^{j}(r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}=\int_{0}^{t}\chi(r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}.

(3.111)

Applying the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality
[
99
, Prop. 2.1]
yields
10
10

10

The stated
p
-dependence holds for all
p\geq 2
in view of
[
99
, Rem. 2.1]
. We absorb the (single) extra case
p=1
into the constant
K
, but remark that one cannot
extend the bound uniformly to
p\in(1,2)
. This is why we restrict our analysis to integer values of
p
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\,\mathcal{I}_{s}[G,\Gamma]^{p}&\leq&p^{p/2}K^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\|\chi(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right]^{p/2}\\[5.69046pt]
&=&p^{p/2}K^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\|\chi^{j}(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right]^{p/2},\end{array}

(3.112)

where we implicitly have used the identity
\mathbf{1}_{[r_{j-1},r_{j})}\mathbf{1}_{[r_{k-1},r_{k})}=\delta_{jk}
. In view of the estimate

\|\chi^{j}(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq K_{\Gamma}^{2}e^{-2\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\|G(r)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}\|B^{j}(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2},

(3.113)

we may write
M_{\varepsilon}[G]=\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-t)}\|G(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}

and compute

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\|\chi^{j}(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq K_{\Gamma}^{2}e^{\varepsilon\delta}M_{\varepsilon}[G]\sum_{j=1}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\|B^{j}(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}r

(3.114)

\displaystyle\leq K_{\Gamma}^{2}e^{2\varepsilon\delta}M_{\varepsilon}[G]\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|B^{j}(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=K_{\Gamma}^{2}e^{2\varepsilon\delta}M_{\varepsilon}[G]\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|B(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}r,

exploiting the disjoint supports of
B^{j}
. The first
estimate in (
3.109
) now follows from the elementary bound

ab\leq\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})
.
The deterministic bound follows in a similar but more straightforward fashion.
∎

As a final preparation, we shall expand the inner products involving pairs of
Y^{j}_{\delta}
. In particular,
treating the inner product parameter
s
as fixed
and by noting that
\nu^{\delta}(s)
is
\mathcal{F}_{r_{j-1}}
-measurable for
r_{j-1}\leq s\leq r_{j}
,
we may apply a mild Itô formula
[
9
]
and find

\displaystyle\langle Y_{\delta}^{j}(s),Y_{\delta}^{j}(s)\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{s}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k},E^{\delta}(t,r)B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k}\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\,\mathrm{d}r

(3.115)

\displaystyle+2\int_{r_{j-1}}^{t}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r)Y_{\delta}^{j}(r),E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)[\,\cdot\,]\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}.

We are now ready to estimate the remaining expressions. We note that

\mathcal{H}_{I;B}
involves a telescoping argument
that only generates cross-terms between neighbouring mesh intervals. Due to the delay
\delta>0
, these can still intuitively be interpreted as uncorrelated.

Lemma 3.15
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.11

together with the partition
\pi
.
Then there exists a constant
K>0
that does not depend on
T
so that for any integer
p\geq 1
we have the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}_{I;B}]^{p}

\displaystyle\leq p^{p/2}K^{p}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left[e^{-\varepsilon(T-t)}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}\right]^{p}

(3.116)

\displaystyle+p^{p/2}K^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|B(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]^{p}.

Proof.

For convenience, we define
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I;B}=-2\mathcal{H}_{I;B}
and use the recurrence relation (
3.99
)
to compute

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I;B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\Big[e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\langle Z_{\delta}(r_{j})-Y_{\delta}^{j}(r_{j}),Z_{\delta}(r_{j})-Y_{\delta}^{j}(r_{j})\rangle_{H^{k}}

(3.117)

\displaystyle-e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j-1})}\langle Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})\rangle_{H^{k}}\Big].

Upon introducing the shorthand notations

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{H}_{I;Ba}&=&\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\langle E^{\delta}(r_{j},r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),Y_{\delta}^{j}(r_{j})\rangle_{H^{k}},\\[5.69046pt]
\mathcal{H}_{I;Bb}&=&\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\langle Y_{\delta}^{j}(r_{j}),Y_{\delta}^{j}(r_{j})\rangle_{H^{k}},\end{array}

(3.118)

we notice that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I;B}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\left[e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\langle Z_{\delta}(r_{j}),Z_{\delta}(r_{j})\rangle_{H^{k}}-e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j-1})}\langle Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})\rangle_{H^{k}}\right]-2\mathcal{H}_{I;Ba}-\mathcal{H}_{I;Bb}

\displaystyle=\langle Z_{\delta}(T),Z_{\delta}(T)\rangle_{H^{k}}-e^{-\varepsilon(T-\delta)}\langle Z_{\delta}(\delta),Z_{\delta}(\delta)\rangle_{H^{k}}-2\mathcal{H}_{I;Ba}-\mathcal{H}_{I;Bb}.

(3.119)

It hence remains for us to establish bounds for
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}_{I;Ba}]^{p}
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}_{I;Bb}]^{p}
that can be absorbed into (
3.116
). Firstly, observe that we have

\mathcal{H}_{I;Ba}=\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\langle E^{\delta}(r_{j},r)E^{\delta}(r,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),E^{\delta}(r_{j},r)B^{j}(r)\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}\rangle_{H^{k}},

(3.120)

which can be estimated accordingly by applying Lemma
3.14
with
G(r)=G_{I;Ba}(r),
where

G_{I;Ba}(r)=E^{\delta}(r,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),\quad r_{j-1}\leq r\leq r_{j}.

(3.121)

Indeed, we see that

\displaystyle e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|G_{I;Ba}(r)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Me^{\varepsilon\delta}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j-1})}\|Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})\|_{H^{k}}^{2}

(3.122)

\displaystyle\leq Me^{\varepsilon\delta}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-t)}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}.

Secondly, using the mild Itô representation (
3.115
)
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{I;Bb}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\langle E^{\delta}(r_{j},r)B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k},E^{\delta}(r_{j},r)B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k}\rangle_{H^{k}}\,\mathrm{d}r

(3.123)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j})}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\langle E^{\delta}(r_{j},r)Y_{\delta}^{j}(r),E^{\delta}(r_{j},r)B^{j}(r)\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}\rangle_{H^{k}}.

Both terms can be treated using Lemma
3.14
again, yet now with
G(r)=G_{I;Bb}(r)
, where

G_{I;Bb}(r)=Y_{\delta}^{j}(r)=Z_{\delta}(r)-E^{\delta}(r,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),\quad r_{j-1}\leq r\leq r_{j}.

(3.124)

Indeed, the latter satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|G_{I;Bb}(r)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|Z_{\delta}(r)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+2Me^{\varepsilon\delta}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r_{j-1})}\|Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1})\|_{H^{k}}^{2}

(3.125)

\displaystyle\leq 2(Me^{\varepsilon\delta}+1)\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-t)}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.11

together with the partition
\pi
.
Then there exists a constant
K>0
that does not depend on
T
so that for any integer
p\geq 1
we have the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}_{II}]^{p}

\displaystyle\leq p^{p/2}K^{p}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big[e^{-\varepsilon(T-t)}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}\Big]^{p}

(3.126)

\displaystyle+p^{p/2}K^{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|B(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big]^{p}.

Proof.

We first note that

\mathcal{H}_{II}=\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\int_{0}^{s}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}s.

(3.127)

Reversing the order of integration yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{II}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\int_{r}^{r_{j}}\ e^{-\varepsilon(T-s)}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)[\,\cdot\,]\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}

(3.128)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\mathcal{J}^{\delta}({r_{j},r})\big[E^{\delta}(r,r_{j-1})Z_{\delta}(r_{j-1}),B^{j}(r)[\,\cdot\,]\big]\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}.

The bound follows by applying Lemma
3.14

with
G(r)
as in (
3.121
).
∎

Lemma 3.17
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.11

together with the partition
\pi
.
Then there exists a constant
K>0
that does not depend on
T
so that for any integer
p\geq 1
we have the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}_{III}]^{p}

\displaystyle\leq p^{p/2}K^{p}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big[e^{-\varepsilon(T-t)}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}\Big]^{p}

(3.129)

\displaystyle+p^{p/2}K^{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\varepsilon(T-r)}\|B(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big]^{p}.

Proof.

Applying the mild Itô representation (
3.115
), we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{III}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{s}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k},E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k}\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.130)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{s}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r)Y_{\delta}^{j}(r),E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Reversing the order of integration gives us

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{III}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\int_{r}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k},E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k}\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}r

(3.131)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\int_{r}^{r_{j}}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\langle E^{\delta}(s,r)Y_{\delta}^{j}(r),E^{\delta}(s,r)B^{j}(r)[\,\cdot\,]\rangle_{s;\delta;k+1}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q},

which can hence be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{III}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\mathcal{J}^{\delta}(r_{j},r)[B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k},B^{j}(r)\sqrt{Q}e_{k}]\,\mathrm{d}r

(3.132)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j=j_{\delta}}^{N}\int_{r_{j-1}}^{r_{j}}\mathcal{J}^{\delta}(r_{j},r)\big[Z^{j}(r),B^{j}(r)[\,\cdot\,]\big]\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{Q}.

The bound now follows by appealing to Lemma
3.14
,
with
G(r)
as in (
3.124
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.11
.

We first consider adapted finite-rank step processes
B

that take values in
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+3})
.
Following step 3 in the proof of
[
70
, Thm. 6.4]
,
the representation
(
3.88
)
can be used to show that
Z_{\delta}(\cdot,\omega)\to\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](\cdot,\omega)
holds in
C([0,T];H^{k+1})
, as
\delta\downarrow 0
, for almost any
\omega\in\Omega
.
Moreover, for these
\omega\in\Omega
we have
the convergence

I_{\delta}(\omega)\to\mathcal{I}^{\perp}_{B}(\omega),

(3.133)

as
\delta\downarrow 0,

since we can find a constant
K_{\omega}>0
so that
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|Z_{\delta}(t,\omega)\|_{H^{k+1}}\leq K_{\omega}
holds for all
small
\delta>0
.

Applying Fatou’s lemma, we see that

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}^{\perp}_{B}]^{p}\leq\liminf_{\delta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{E}[I_{\delta}]^{p}.

(3.134)

Clearly, the convergence of
Z_{\delta}
to
\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B]
also holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. in
C([0,T];H^{k})
. Recalling the bound (
3.89
),
where the right hand side is contained in
L^{2p}(\Omega)
, we may apply the dominated convergence theorem to
show that

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|^{2p}_{H^{k}}\to\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p},

(3.135)

as
\delta\downarrow 0
, together with

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\big[e^{-\varepsilon(T-t)}\|Z_{\delta}(t)\|^{2}_{H^{k}}\big]^{p}\to\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\big[e^{-\varepsilon(T-t)}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}\big]^{p}.

(3.136)

The desired bound follows by
appealing to
(
3.71
) and combining
Lemma’s
3.13
,

3.15
,

3.16

and

3.17
.
In a standard fashion, the result can be extended
to general processes
B
satisfying (
3.83
), by means of
Corollary
3.8
.
∎

3.5
Bounding suprema with forward integrals logarithmically in time

In this section, we examine how fast the unweighted supremum over
[0,T]
of the convolution
\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B]
grows when increasing
T
. For instance, if we assume that
B
is constant and take
\varepsilon=0

in (
3.72
), we obtain a prediction of order
T^{p}
.
Our main result here states that this can be improved to yield growth estimates for the expressions

\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)=\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)P^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B](t)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(3.137)

that are logarithmic in
T
. This will be achieved by
imposing the following a priori pathwise bounds on
B
,
which will arise in a natural fashion during our stability analysis in §
7
.

(HB)

For the process
B\in\mathcal{N}^{2}\big([0,T];\mathbb{F};HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})\big)

there exists a constant
\Theta_{*}>0
such that
the pathwise bounds

\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq\Theta_{*}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\|E_{\rm tw}(1)B(t)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\leq\Theta_{*}^{2}

(3.138)

hold for all
0\leq t\leq T
.

Observe that (
3.138
) in (HB) implies that
B\in\mathcal{N}^{p}\big([0,T];\mathbb{F};HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})\big)
for all
p\geq 2.
We recall that the constant
\mu=\min\{\beta,\lambda_{1}k_{\nu}\}>0
is defined for the first time in Lemma
3.2
.

Proposition 3.18
.

Suppose that
(HE)
holds. Assume that
T\geq 2
is an integer and pick
\varepsilon\in(0,2\mu)
.
Then there exists a constant
K_{\rm gr}>0
that does not depend on
T

so that for any process
B
that satisfies
(HB)
, and every integer
p\geq 1
,
we have the growth bound

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)\right\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2p}+\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B](t)^{p}\leq K^{2p}_{\rm gr}\Theta_{*}^{2p}(p^{p}+\log(T)^{p}).

(3.139)

We will primarily follow the approach of

[
29
, Sec. 3.1]
,
but the forward integrals require subtle modifications to the procedure at several points, which we highlight below.
In addition, our bound for
\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B]
is sharper than the related bound in
[
29
]

due to Proposition
3.11
. The approach
relies on integral splittings, which
are only available for finite-rank processes. We will therefore first impose the following condition and use a limiting argument in the final step to lift the restriction.

(HB*)

The process
B
satisfies condition (HB) and, in addition, is a finite-rank process that takes values in
HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k+2})
.

Under this assumption, we
can use (
3.49
)
to make the splitting

\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](t)=\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}(t)+\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{sh}}(t),

(3.140)

as in
[
29
]
.
Here the long time (lt) and short time (sh) contributions are given by

\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}(t)=\int_{0}^{t-1}E(t,s)P^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{sh}}(t)=\int_{t-1}^{t}E(t,s)P^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-},

(3.141)

respectively, where we interpret the boundary
t-1
as
\max\{t-1,0\}
if necessary. Both these terms need to be handled using separate techniques.

Lemma 3.19

(short-term bound)
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.18
.
Then there exists a constant
K_{\rm sh}>0

that does not depend on
T

so that for any process
B
that satisfies

(HB*)
, and every integer
p\geq 1
, we have the supremum bound

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\rm sh}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq(p^{p}+\log(T)^{p})K_{\rm sh}^{2p}\Theta_{*}^{2p}.

(3.142)

Proof.

The result is obtained by generalising
[
29
, Lem. 3.4]
in a completely analogous way, followed by
using the estimate on maximum expectations in either Corollary
B.3
or
[
29
, Cor. 2.4]
.
∎

For any pair
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq T
, we split
the long term increment into two parts:

\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}(t_{1})-\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}(t_{2})=\mathcal{I}_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})+\mathcal{I}_{2}(t_{1},t_{2}),

(3.143)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})=\int_{0}^{t_{1}-1}[E(t_{2},s)-E(t_{1},s)]P^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-},

(3.144)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})=\int_{t_{1}-1}^{t_{2}-1}E(t_{2},s)P^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}.

(3.145)

Note that this is in line with
[
29
]
.
The first integral can be analysed by exploiting the regularity of the random evolution family
E(t,s)
for
t-s\geq 1
. The second integral requires a supremum bound on the smoothened process
E_{\rm tw}(1)B
, which motivates the condition in (
3.138
).

Lemma 3.20
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.18

and assume that
(HB*)
holds. Then for any
1\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq T
and any integer
p\geq 1
we have the bound

\mathbb{E}\left\|\mathcal{I}_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq p^{p}e^{\varepsilon p}K_{\mathrm{dc}}^{2p}M^{4p}\Theta_{*}^{2p}\left|t_{2}-t_{1}\right|^{2p}.

(3.146)

Proof.

Observe first that by Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\|\mathcal{I}_{1}\left(t_{1},t_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\left[E\left(t_{2},t_{1}\right)-I\right]E(t_{1},t_{1}-1)\right\|_{\mathscr{L}\left(H^{k}\right)}^{2p}\left\|\int_{0}^{t_{1}-1}E(t_{1}-1,s)P^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq M^{2p}\left|t_{2}-t_{1}\right|^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left\|\int_{0}^{t_{1}-1}E\left(t_{1}-1,s\right)P^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

(3.147)

\displaystyle\leq M^{4p}\left|t_{2}-t_{1}\right|^{2p}p^{p}K_{\mathrm{dc}}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t_{1}-1}e^{-\varepsilon\left(t_{1}-1-s\right)}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}.

The second inequality exploits the fact that
\|(E(t,r)-I)E(r,r-1)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}\leq M|t-r|
holds, which is a generalised version of the final estimate in
[
29
, Lem 2.1]
. The ultimate inequality follows from (
3.72
) in Proposition
3.10
. The assertion follows in view of (
3.138
).
∎

We remind the reader that
E(t,s)=E(t,s,\omega)
depends on
\omega\in\Omega.
Consequently, the proof of the lemma below is slightly different as compared to
[
29
, Lem 3.5]
. In particular, we need the expanded decomposition (
3.22
).

Lemma 3.21
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.18

and assume that
(HB*)
holds.
Then for any
1\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq T
and any integer
p\geq 1
we have the bound

\mathbb{E}\left\|\mathcal{I}_{2}(t_{1},t_{2}\right)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq p^{p}K_{\mathrm{dc}}^{2p}\|P^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}^{2p}M^{2p}\Theta_{*}^{2p}\left|t_{2}-t_{1}\right|^{p}.

(3.148)

Proof.

An application of Cauchy-Schwarz yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

(3.149)

\displaystyle\leq\|P^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|E(t_{2},t_{2}-1)\int_{t_{1}-1}^{t_{2}-1}E(t_{2}-1,s)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}\right]^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq\|P^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\|H(t_{2},t_{2}-1)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\int_{t_{1}-1}^{t_{2}-1}E(t_{2}-1,s)E_{\rm tw}(1)B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}\right]^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq\|P^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}^{2p}M^{2p}p^{p}K_{\mathrm{dc}}^{2p}\left|t_{2}-t_{1}\right|^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t_{1}-1\leq s\leq t_{2}-1}\|E_{\rm tw}(1)B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\right]^{p}.

By applying (
3.138
), we hence arrive at the stated bound.∎

The previous two results give a handle on small increments
\left|t_{2}-t_{1}\right|\leq 1
. For larger increments, one simply exploits the decay of the random evolution family.

Lemma 3.22
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.18

and assume that
(HB*)
holds.
Then for any
0\leq t\leq T
and any integer
p\geq 1
we have the bound

\mathbb{E}\left\|\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}(t)\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq p^{p}e^{\varepsilon p}K_{\mathrm{dc}}^{2p}M^{2p}\Theta_{*}^{2p}.

(3.150)

Proof.

Using the decay estimate (
3.72
) in Proposition
3.10
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\|\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}(t)\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq\|E(t,t-1)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left\|\int_{0}^{t-1}E(t-1,s)P^{\perp}B(s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}\right\|_{H^{k}}

(3.151)

\displaystyle\leq M^{2p}p^{p}K_{\mathrm{dc}}^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t-1}e^{-\varepsilon(t-1-s)}\|B(s)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}.

The assertion again follows from (
3.138
).
∎

Corollary 3.23
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.18

and assume that
(HB*)
holds.
Then for any
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq T
and any integer
p\geq 1
we have the bound

\mathbb{E}\left\|\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}\left(t_{1}\right)-\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}\left(t_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq 2^{2p}p^{p}e^{\varepsilon p}K_{\mathrm{dc}}^{2p}\|P^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}^{2p}M^{4p}\Theta_{*}^{2p}\min\left\{\left|t_{2}-t_{1}\right|^{1/2},1\right\}^{2p}.

(3.152)

Proof.

This follows from the standard inequality
(a+b)^{2p}\leq 2^{2p-1}\left(a^{2p}+b^{2p}\right)
and a combination of the estimates from Lemma
3.20
, Lemma
3.21
, and Lemma
3.22
.
∎

As in
[
29
]
, we will proceed by using the results from Dirksen
[
13
]
, which is again based on the chaining principle developed by Talagrand
[
91
]
.

Lemma 3.24

(long-term bound)
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3.18
.
Then there exists a constant
K_{\mathrm{lt}}>0

that does not depend on
T
so that
for any process
B
that satisfies
(HB*)
, and any integer
p\geq 1
, we have the supremum bound

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}(t)\right\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq\left(p^{p}+\log(T)^{p}\right)K_{\mathrm{lt}}^{2p}\Theta_{*}^{2p}.

(3.153)

Proof.

Upon writing

d_{\max}=2\sqrt{e}K_{\mathrm{dc}}M^{2}\|P^{\perp}\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{k})}\Theta_{*}
together with

d\left(t_{1},t_{2}\right)=d_{\max}\min\left\{\sqrt{\left|t_{2}-t_{1}\right|},1\right\},

(3.154)

an application of
[
29
, Lemma 2.2]
to Corollary
3.23
provides the bound

P\left(\left\|\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}\left(t_{1}\right)-\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\mathrm{lt}}\left(t_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{k}}>\vartheta\right)\leq 2\exp\left[-\frac{\vartheta^{2}}{2d\left(t_{1},t_{2}\right)^{2}}\right].

(3.155)

Furthermore, recall that the map
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\rm lt}(t)\in H^{k}

has continuous paths
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely, as a consequence of Proposition
3.5
. This
suffices to establish the equality

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\rm lt}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}=\textstyle\sup_{K\subset[0,T],|K|<\infty}\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in K}\|\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\rm lt}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p},

(3.156)

consequently allowing us to use the results in
[
13
]
, since these are only stated for suprema over sets of finite cardinality.
In particular, for any integer
p\geq 1
, there is a constant
C_{\rm ch}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\mathcal{E}_{B}^{\rm lt}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\leq C_{\rm ch}^{2p}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log(N(T,d,u))}\,\mathrm{d}u\right]^{2p}+C_{\rm ch}^{2p}d_{\rm max}^{2p}p^{p},

(3.157)

where
N(T,d,u)
denotes the smallest number of intervals of length at most
u>0
, in the metric
d
, required to cover the interval
[0,T].

This follows by choosing
\alpha=2
in
[
13
, eq. (3.2)]
, together with
[
13
, eq. (2.3)]
, and by applying the final inequality in the proof of
[
13
, Thm. 3.2]
.

Conform to the proof in
[
29
, Lem. 3.9]
, the
Dudley entropy integral can be bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log(N(T,d,u))}\,\mathrm{d}u\leq d_{\rm max}(\sqrt{2\log(T)}+\sqrt{\pi})\leq 4d_{\rm max}\sqrt{\log(T)},

(3.158)

as
T\geq 2.

The desired estimate now follows directly.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.18
.

Combining the short time result in Lemma
3.19
with the long time result in Lemma
3.24
yields the supremum bound for
\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B]
for processes that satisfy (HB*). A standard limiting argument
using Corollary
3.8
then generalises the bound to processes that satisfy (HB).

Turning towards the supremum bound for
\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B]
, note that
for any
0\leq t\leq T,
Proposition
3.11
implies that
for every integer
p\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\,\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B](t)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq K^{p}\,\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B](r)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\big]

(3.159)

\displaystyle+p^{p/2}K^{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-r)}\|B(r)\|_{HS(\mathcal{W}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\,dr\Big]^{p},

for some
K>0
. Using the estimate
(
3.139
) for
\mathcal{Z}^{\perp}[B]
which we have just established, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\,\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B](t)^{p}\leq K^{p}\Theta_{*}^{2p}(p^{p}+\log(T)^{p})+p^{p/2}K^{p}\Theta_{*}^{2p}\leq p^{p}\Theta_{1}^{p}+\Theta_{2}^{p},

(3.160)

for some updated
K>0
, with
\Theta_{1}=2K\Theta_{*}^{2}
and
\Theta_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\Theta_{1}\log(T).

Corollary
B.3
now implies

\mathbb{E}\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,T\}}\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B](i)^{p}\leq K^{p}\Theta_{*}^{2p}(p^{p}+\log(T)^{p}),

(3.161)

after updating
K>0
again.
To conclude the proof, it suffices to observe
that

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B](t)

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,T\}}\sup_{i-1\leq t\leq i}\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B](t)\leq e^{\varepsilon}\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,T\}}\mathcal{I}^{\perp}[B](i)

(3.162)

holds for any integer
T\geq 2
.
∎

4
Bounds on nonlinearities

In this section, we obtain estimates on the nonlinearities that we encounter throughout this paper.
We start in §
4.1
with some useful preparatory bounds, proceeding
in §
4.2
with estimates that hold in
L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. As a consequence
of our pointwise global Lipschitz assumptions, we will be able to extract more information than was possible in
[
27
,
30
]
.
We proceed in §
4.3
and §
4.4
with
H^{k}
-based estimates for the terms appearing in our stochastic and deterministic expressions, respectively. Finally, in §
4.5
we consider the low dimensional setting
1\leq d\leq 4
under the cubic growth condition (Hf-Cub). We remind the reader that the full list of functions can be found in Appendix
A
. Recall that we abbreviate the notation for function spaces only if the domain is
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
and the codomain is
\mathbb{R}^{n}.

4.1
Preliminaries

Let us start by considering
H^{k}
-based estimates for
the Nemytskii operators that we use. For this, we consider a sufficiently smooth function
\Theta:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}

and assume that
\Phi
is bounded and sufficiently smooth on
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
.
As an illustration, we compute

\displaystyle\partial_{x}[\Theta(\Phi+v_{A})-\Theta(\Phi+v_{B})]

\displaystyle=D\Theta(\Phi+v_{A})[\partial_{x}v_{A}-\partial_{x}v_{B}]

(4.1)

\displaystyle+\big(D\Theta(\Phi+v_{A})-D\Theta(\Phi+v_{B}))[\partial_{x}\Phi+\partial_{x}v_{B}].

Since
\Phi
is bounded,
this provides the pointwise estimate

|\partial_{x}[\Theta(\Phi+v_{A})-\Theta(\Phi+v_{B})]|\leq K\Big(|\partial_{x}v_{A}-\partial_{x}v_{B}|+|v_{A}-v_{B}|(|\partial_{x}\Phi|+|\partial_{x}v_{B}|)\Big)

(4.2)

under the assumption that
\Theta
and
D\Theta

are globally Lipschitz,
which automatically implies that
D\Theta
is bounded.
We can hence not expect global Lipschitz bounds to hold in
H^{k}
and the cross-terms will rapidly become more involved as
k
is increased. To control these expressions, we recall that for any
k>d/2

we can find a constant
K>0
so that
for any bounded
\ell
-linear map
\Lambda:(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
, the bound

\|\Lambda[\partial^{\alpha_{1}}v_{1},\ldots,\partial^{\alpha_{\ell}}v_{\ell}]\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq K|\Lambda|\|v_{1}\|_{H^{k}}\cdots\|v_{\ell}\|_{H^{k}}

(4.3)

holds for any tuple
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in(H^{k})^{\ell}
, provided that
|\alpha_{1}|+\ldots+|\alpha_{\ell}|\leq k
. This is related to the fact that
H^{k}
is an algebra under multiplication, i.e.,
\|vw\|_{H^{k}}\leq K\|v\|_{H^{k}}\|w\|_{H^{k}}
, for
k>d/2
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Pick
k>d/2
, assume that
\Phi
is bounded with
\Phi^{\prime}\in H^{k}
, and consider a
C^{k}
-smooth function
\Theta:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
for which
D^{\ell}\Theta

is globally Lipschitz for all
0\leq\ell\leq k
. Then
there exists a constant
K>0
so that for each pair
v_{A},v_{B}\in H^{k}

we have the bound

\|\Theta(\Phi+v_{A})-\Theta(\Phi+v_{B})\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq K(1+\|v_{A}\|_{H^{k}}^{k}+\|v_{B}\|_{H^{k}}^{k})\|v_{A}-v_{B}\|_{H^{k}}.

(4.4)

Proof.

Consider a multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{\geq 0}

with
|\alpha|\leq k
.
We now claim that it is possible to write the spatial
derivative
\partial^{\alpha}[\Theta(\Phi+v_{A})-\Theta(\Phi+v_{B})]

as a finite sum of expressions of two types.
The first type is given by

\mathcal{I}_{I}=D^{\ell}\Theta(\Phi+v_{A})[\partial^{\beta_{1}}(v_{A}-v_{B}),\partial^{\beta_{2}}(\Phi+v_{\#_{1}}),\ldots,\partial^{\beta_{\ell}}(\Phi+v_{\#_{\ell}})],

(4.5)

with
\#_{i}\in\{A,B\}
and multi-indices
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{\geq 0}
that satisfy
|\beta_{i}|\geq 1
, for each
1\leq i\leq\ell\leq|\alpha|
, together with
|\beta_{1}|+\ldots+|\beta_{\ell}|=|\alpha|
.
The second type is given by

\mathcal{I}_{II}=\Big(D^{\ell}\Theta(\Phi+v_{A})-D^{\ell}\Theta(\Phi+v_{B})\Big)\Big[\partial^{\beta_{1}}(\Phi+v_{B}),\dots,\partial^{\beta_{\ell}}(\Phi+v_{B})\Big],

(4.6)

with the same conditions on
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
.
This can be readily verified with induction.

Using (
4.3
)
together with the global Lipschitz properties of
\Theta
, which automatically imply that
D^{\ell}\Theta
is bounded for
1\leq\ell\leq k
,
we obtain the bounds

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|\mathcal{I}_{I}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\leq&K\|v_{A}-v_{B}\|_{H^{k}}\big[1+\|v_{A}\|^{k-1}_{H^{k}}+\|v_{B}\|^{k-1}_{H^{k}}\big],\\[5.69046pt]
\|\mathcal{I}_{II}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\leq&K\|v_{A}-v_{B}\|_{\infty}\big[1+\|v_{B}\|^{k}_{H^{k}}\big].\end{array}

(4.7)

Both terms can be absorbed in (
4.4
)
in view of the Sobolev embedding
H^{k}\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}
.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Pick
k>d/2
, assume that
\Phi
is bounded with
\Phi^{\prime}\in H^{k+1}
, and consider a
C^{k+1}
-smooth function
\Theta:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
for which
D^{\ell}\Theta

is globally Lipschitz for all
0\leq\ell\leq k+1
. Then
there exists a constant
K>0
so that for each pair
v_{A},v_{B}\in H^{k+1}

we have the bound

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|\Theta(\Phi+v_{A})-\Theta(\Phi+v_{B})\|_{H^{k+1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})}&\leq&K(1+\|v_{A}\|_{H^{k}}^{k}+\|v_{B}\|_{H^{k}}^{k})\\[5.69046pt]
&&\qquad\qquad\times\big(1+\|v_{A}\|_{H^{k+1}}+\|v_{B}\|_{H^{k+1}}\big)\\[5.69046pt]
&&\qquad\qquad\times\|v_{A}-v_{B}\|_{H^{k}}\\[5.69046pt]
&&\qquad+K(1+\|v_{A}\|_{H^{k}}^{k}+\|v_{B}\|_{H^{k}}^{k})\\[5.69046pt]
&&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\|v_{A}-v_{B}\|_{H^{k+1}}.\end{array}

(4.8)

Proof.

Inspecting the terms
\mathcal{I}_{I}

and
\mathcal{I}_{II}

in (
4.5
)
and (
4.6
),
but where now
|\alpha|=k+1
is also allowed,
we see that each term can be covered by appealing
to the bound in (
4.3
).
It is only necessary to replace one of the
v_{i}

in this bound
by the differentiated version
\partial^{\gamma}v_{i}

for some multi-index
\gamma
that has a single component, i.e.,
|\gamma|=1
. This leads directly to the stated estimates.
∎

When we additionally have
\Theta(\Phi)\in H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})
, we may use (
4.4
) to obtain the bound

\|\Theta(\Phi+v)\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq K\big[1+\|v\|_{H^{k}}^{k+1}\big],

(4.9)

for all
v\in H^{k}
, possibly after increasing
K
. In the same fashion, whenever
\Theta(\Phi)\in H^{k+1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})
, we may use (
4.8
)
to obtain

\|\Theta(\Phi+v)\|_{H^{k+1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq K\big[1+\|v\|_{H^{k}}^{k+1}\big]\big[1+\|v\|_{H^{k+1}}\big],

(4.10)

for all
v\in H^{k+1}
.
This will be the case when we consider the functions
f
,
g
and
h
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Pick
k>d/2
, assume that
\Phi
is bounded with
\Phi^{\prime}\in H^{k}
, and consider a
C^{k+2}
-smooth function
\Theta:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
for which
D^{\ell}\Theta

is globally Lipschitz for all
0\leq\ell\leq k+2
. Then
there exists a constant
K>0
so that for every
v\in H^{k}

we have the bound

\|\Theta(\Phi+v)-\Theta(\Phi)-D\Theta(\Phi)[v]\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq K(1+\|v\|_{H^{k}}^{k})\|v\|^{2}_{H^{k}}.

(4.11)

Proof.

Note first that we have the pointwise representation

\displaystyle\Theta(\Phi+v)-\Theta(\Phi)-D\Theta(\Phi)[v]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\Big(D\Theta(\Phi+tv)[v]-D\Theta(\Phi)[v]\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t

(4.12)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\left(t\int_{0}^{1}D^{2}\Theta(\Phi+stv)[v,v]\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathrm{d}t.

Consider a multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{\geq 0}

with
|\alpha|\leq k
.
We now claim that the spatial
derivative

\partial^{\alpha}D^{2}\Theta(\Phi+stv)[v,v]

can be written as a finite sum of expressions of the form

\mathcal{I}=D^{\ell+2}\Theta(\Phi+stv)\Big[\partial^{\beta_{1}}(\Phi+stv),\partial^{\beta_{2}}(\Phi+stv),\ldots,\partial^{\beta_{\ell+1}}v,\partial^{\beta_{\ell+2}}v\Big],

(4.13)

with multi-indices
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell+2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{\geq 0}

that satisfy
|\beta_{i}|\geq 1
, for each
1\leq i\leq\ell+2\leq|\alpha|
,
together with
|\beta_{1}|+\ldots+|\beta_{\ell+2}|=|\alpha|
. This can again readily be verified using induction. Using the global Lipschitz properties,
we obtain the bound

\|\mathcal{I}\|_{L^{2}}\leq K[1+\|v\|_{H^{k}}^{k}]\|v\|_{H^{k}}^{2},

(4.14)

as desired.
∎

We now turn to preliminary observations regarding the Hilbert-Schmidt norms of operators that map
L^{2}_{Q}
into
H^{k}
, extending
the previous results obtained in
[
30
]
for
d=1
. Assuming that (Hq) is satisfied, we can (formally) introduce the function
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}

by taking the Fourier inverse of
\sqrt{\hat{q}}
. We first show that this map is well-defined, confirming that indeed

\hat{p}=\sqrt{\hat{q}}
and hence that
p
can be interpreted as the convolution kernel for
\sqrt{Q}:L^{2}_{Q}\to L^{2}
, i.e.,
\sqrt{Q}v=p*v
for
v\in L^{2}_{Q}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Pick
k\geq 0
and assume that
(Hq)
is satisfied.
Then
p
is well-defined and, in addition, we have
p\in H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})
.

Proof.

Pick a multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{\geq 0}
with
|\alpha|\leq k

and
write
\textbf{z}^{\alpha}=z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots z_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}
.
Applying Cauchy-Schwartz yields

\int_{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{2\alpha}|\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}=\int_{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{2\alpha}}{(1+|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2})^{\ell/2}}(1+|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2})^{\ell/2}|\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq K(\alpha,\ell)\|q\|_{H^{\ell}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})},

(4.15)

in which we have introduced the expression

K(\alpha,\ell)=\int_{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{4\alpha}}{(1+|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2})^{\ell}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{(\omega,\xi)^{4\alpha}}{(1+\omega^{2}+|\xi|^{2})^{\ell}}\mathrm{d}\omega.

(4.16)

We now claim that
K(\alpha,\ell)<\infty
, since
\ell>2|\alpha|+d/2
by assumption (Hq). To see this, note that

K(\alpha,\ell)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\textbf{z}^{4\alpha}}{(1+|\mathbf{z}|^{2})^{\ell}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\textbf{z}|^{4|\alpha|}}{(1+|\mathbf{z}|^{2})^{\ell}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}.

(4.17)

Introducing the spherical coordinate
r=|z|
with the associated integration factor
r^{d-1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\textbf{z}|^{4|\alpha|}}{(1+|\mathbf{z}|^{2})^{\ell}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=V_{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{4|\alpha|}}{(1+r^{2})^{\ell}}r^{d-1}\mathrm{d}r

(4.18)

\displaystyle=V_{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{2|\alpha|+d/2-1}}{(1+u)^{\ell}}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=V_{d}\frac{\Gamma(\ell-2|\alpha|-\tfrac{d}{2})\Gamma(2|\alpha|+\tfrac{d}{2})}{\Gamma(\ell)},

where
\Gamma
denotes the Gamma-function and where
V_{d}={2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}/\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}
indicates the volume of the unit
d
-sphere.

Taking
\alpha=0
, we see that

\boldsymbol{\xi}\mapsto\sqrt{\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{\xi})}
is in
L^{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})
.
This allows us to take the Fourier inverse, ensuring
that
p
is a well-defined map.
Lastly,
Plancherel’s identity (see Appendix
C
) implies

\displaystyle\|p\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}p\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})}^{2}

(4.19)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\left\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\alpha}\hat{p}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\right\|_{L^{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\int_{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{2\alpha}|\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi},

confirming that indeed
p\in H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Pick
k\geq 0
and suppose that
(Hq)
is satisfied. Then
there exists a constant
K>0
so that
any
z\in H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n\times m})
can be interpreted as a Hilbert-Schmidt operator from
L^{2}_{Q}
into
H^{k}
that acts via the pointwise multiplication
z[w](x,y)=z(x,y)w(x,y)
and admits the bound

\|z\|_{HS(L^{2}_{Q};H^{k})}\leq K\|z\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n\times m})}.

(4.20)

Proof.

Without loss of generality we take
n=m=1
to ease the notational complexity.
Consider an orthonormal basis
(e_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}
for
L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})

and introduce the functions

p^{(\ell)}(x,y)=[\sqrt{Q}e_{\ell}](x,y)=\langle p(x-\cdot,y-\cdot),e_{\ell}(\cdot,\cdot)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})},

(4.21)

together with the notation

z_{\ell}(x,y)=z[\sqrt{Q}e_{\ell}](x,y)=z(x,y)[\sqrt{Q}e_{\ell}](x,y)=z(x,y)p^{(\ell)}(x,y).

(4.22)

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\|z\|_{HS(L^{2}_{Q};H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}))}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\|z_{\ell}\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{D}}|\partial^{\alpha}z_{\ell}(x,y)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{D}}\left|\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!(\alpha-\beta)!}\partial^{\beta}z(x,y)\partial^{\alpha-\beta}p^{(\ell)}(x,y)\right|^{2}.

Because
(e_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}
is an orthonormal basis, and due to the translation invariance of the integral, we obtain

\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}|\partial^{\alpha-\beta}p^{(\ell)}(x,y)|^{2}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\langle\partial^{\alpha-\beta}p(x-\cdot,y-\cdot),e_{\ell}(\cdot,\cdot)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\|\partial^{\alpha-\beta}p\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}^{2},

which is independent of the coordinate
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}
. This allows us to compute

\displaystyle\|z\|_{HS(L^{2}_{Q};H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(k!)^{3}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\int_{\mathcal{D}}|\partial^{\alpha}z(x,y)|^{2}|\partial^{\alpha-\beta}p^{(\ell)}(x,y)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

(4.23)

\displaystyle=(k!)^{3}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\int_{\mathcal{D}}|\partial^{\alpha}z(x,y)|^{2}\|\partial^{\alpha-\beta}p\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq(k!)^{4}\|p\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{D}}|\partial^{\alpha}z(x,y)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=(k!)^{4}\|p\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}^{2}\|z\|_{H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R})}^{2},

which in view of Lemma
4.4
completes the proof.
∎

4.2
Bounds in
L^{2}

The pointwise global Lipschitz bounds on our nonlinearities
f
,
g
and
h
will enable us to obtain estimates for the
cut-offs
\chi_{h}
and
\chi_{l}
and the scalar functions
a_{\sigma}
,
b
and
\kappa_{\sigma}
that are entirely based on
L^{2}
-norms. In fact, several of the estimates obtained in previous work for
d=1
