\bar{v}(t)=E(t,0)\bar{v}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(\bar{v}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\bar{v}(s))\mathrm{d}\overline{W}_{s}^{-},

(6.30)

which holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
0\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\bar{v}
is in
C([0,T];L^{2})
. For convenience, we introduce the shorthand notations

\textbf{f}(t)=\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(\bar{v}(t))\quad\text{and}\quad\textbf{g}(t)=\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\bar{v}(t)),

(6.31)

and define the stochastic process
\bar{w}=(\bar{w}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
by

\bar{w}(t)=E(t,0)\bar{v}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)\textbf{f}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)\textbf{g}(s)\mathrm{d}\overline{W}_{s}^{-},\quad 0\leq t\leq T.

(6.32)

By inspecting the definitions (
A.28
) for
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}
and appealing to Corollary
3.8
, we can conclude that
\bar{w}
is well-defined and a continuous
L^{2}
-valued process on
[0,T]
as well.

By a slight adaptation of the proof in
[
70
, Thm. 6.6]
,
using the proof of
[
21
, Thm. 3.2]

to take into account the deterministic term
f
,
one can show that
\bar{w}
is an analytically weak solution
in
L^{2}
to the linear problem

\mathrm{d}z=[\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t)z+\textbf{f}(t)]\mathrm{d}t+\textbf{g}(t)\mathrm{d}\overline{W}_{t}^{Q},

(6.33)

which means pathwise that

\langle\bar{w}(t),\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\bar{w}(0),\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\bar{w}(s),\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{adj}}_{\nu}(s)\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\textbf{f}(s),\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\textbf{g}(s)\mathrm{d}\overline{W}_{s}^{Q},\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}}

(6.34)

holds for all
\zeta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
0\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{\infty}
.
On the other hand, Proposition
6.3
implies that
\bar{v}
is also an analytically weak solution to (
6.33
) on
[0,\bar{\tau}_{\infty})
.
Upon defining
\bar{\phi}=\bar{v}-\bar{w}
, we observe that
\bar{\phi}
solves the linear initial value problem

\partial_{t}\phi=\mathcal{L}_{\nu}(t)\phi,\quad\phi(0)=0,

(6.35)

on
[0,\bar{\tau}_{\infty})
,
which clearly only has the zero solution
\phi=0
.
We therefore see that
\bar{v}=\bar{w}
holds with equality in
L^{2}
on
[0,\bar{\tau}_{\infty})
,
which means that they are equal almost everywhere on this interval. Since
\bar{v}
is known to take values in
H^{k}
on this interval, we conclude that
\bar{w}
does the same and that we have equality in this space, completing the proof.
∎

7
Nonlinear stability

In this section we prove the estimate in Proposition
2.5
, which leads to the stochastic metastability of planar waves over exponentially long timescales as described in Theorem
2.6
. From this point onward, we replace the mild form in (
6.30
) for the time transformed perturbation with the generic equation

w(t)=E(t,0)w(0)+\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)[\sigma^{2}F_{\rm lin}(w(s))+F_{\rm nl}(w(s))]\mathrm{d}s+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)B(w(s))\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-},

(7.1)

in which
E(t,s)
is any random evolution family that is covered by the results in §
3.2
.
Throughout this section we assume there exists a continuous process
w:[0,\infty)\times\Omega\to H^{k}
for some
k\geq 0
and a stopping time
\tau_{\infty}

so that equation (
7.1
) is satisfied for
0\leq t<\tau_{\infty}
.

The maps

F_{\rm lin}:H^{k+1}\to H^{k},\quad F_{\rm nl}:H^{k+1}\to H^{k},\quad B:H^{k+1}\to HS(L^{2}_{Q};H^{k}),

(7.2)

are assumed to satisfy the bounds

\displaystyle\|F_{\rm lin}(w)\|_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq K_{\rm lin}\|w\|_{H^{k+1}},

(7.3)

\displaystyle\|F_{\rm nl}(w)\|_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq K_{\rm nl}\|w\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2},

(7.4)

\displaystyle\|B(w)\|_{HS(L^{2}_{Q},H^{k})}

\displaystyle\leq K_{B}(1+\|w\|_{H^{k+1}}),

(7.5)

whenever
\|w\|_{H^{k}}\leq 1
.
In addition, if
\|w\|_{L^{2}}\leq\eta_{0}
holds for some sufficiently small
\eta_{0}>0
, then we have the orthogonal identities

\langle\sigma F_{\rm lin}(w)+F_{\rm nl}(w),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\langle B(w)[\xi],\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0.

(7.6)

These choices reflect the bounds that arise from (Hf-Cub); see Corollary
4.31
. Of course, they also cover the case where (Hf-Lip) holds, but the computations in Lemma
7.4
could be simplified slightly by replacing (
7.4
) with
\|F_{\rm nl}(w)\|_{H^{k}}\leq K_{\rm nl}\|w\|_{H^{k}}\|w\|_{H^{k+1}}
; see Corollaries

4.15
and
4.21
. Lastly, we refer to the remarks on the orthogonality conditions in Appendix
A
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,2\mu)
with
\mu
as in Lemma
3.2
. Recall that our main objective is to control the size of

N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)=\|w(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|w(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s.

(7.7)

In particular, for any
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
, we write

t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)=\inf\{t\geq 0:\|w(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|w(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s>\eta\}

(7.8)

for the associated stopping time and observe that the conditions in (
7.6
) are automatically satisfied for
w=w(t)
with
t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)
. In addition, the definition of the localising sequence (
5.21
) implies
t_{\mathrm{st}}(\eta;k)<\tau_{\infty}
. Finally, note that
\langle w(t),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
for
t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)
if and only if
\langle w(0),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
.

Our main result here provides logarithmic growth bounds
for the expectation of the maximal value that
N_{\varepsilon;k}
attains as we increase
T
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Consider the generic setting above. Pick two sufficiently small constants
\delta_{\eta}>0
and
\delta_{\sigma}>0
. Then there exists a constant
K>0
so that for any integer
T\geq 2,
any
0<\eta<\delta_{\eta},
any
0\leq\sigma\leq\delta_{\sigma},
and any integer
p\geq 1
, we have the bound

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}|N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)|^{p}\right]\leq K^{p}\left[\|v(0)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}+\sigma^{2p}\big(p^{p}+\log(T)^{p}\big)\right].

(7.9)

Remark 7.2
.

The estimates obtained in
[
27
,
28
,
30
]

for
d=1
also fit into the generic framework of this section upon choosing
k=0
. In particular, Proposition
7.1
sharpens
[
29
, Prop. 5.1]
,
which means that the bound (
2.60
) extends
to this setting and improves
[
29
, Thm. 1.1]
.

Following the earlier work
[
27
]
,
we proceed by providing separate estimates for the integrals in (
7.1
). To this end, we introduce the notation

\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)=E(t,0)P^{\perp}w(0),

(7.10)

together with the integrals

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{F;\text{lin}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)P^{\perp}F_{\operatorname{lin}}(w(s))1_{s<t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)}\mathrm{d}s,

(7.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{F;\mathrm{nl}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)P^{\perp}F_{\mathrm{nl}}(w(s))1_{s<t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)}\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{B}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}E(t,s)P^{\perp}B(w(s))1_{s<t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)}\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{-}.

The presence of the projection
P^{\perp}
in the above is simply to emphasise condition (
7.6
). Using these expressions, we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}\|w(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

(7.12)

\displaystyle\leq 4^{2p}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left[\|\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}+\sigma^{4p}\|\mathcal{E}_{F;\rm lin}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}+\|\mathcal{E}_{F;\rm nl}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}+\sigma^{2p}\|\mathcal{E}_{B}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}\right].

Turning to the integrated
H^{k+1}
-bound, we introduce the integrals

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|\mathcal{E}_{0}(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

(7.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{F;\mathrm{lin}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|\mathcal{E}_{F;\mathrm{lin}}(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

(7.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{F;\mathrm{nl}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|\mathcal{E}_{F;\mathrm{nl}}(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

(7.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{B}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|\mathcal{E}_{B}(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s.

(7.16)

This leads directly to the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}\left[\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|w(s)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{p}

(7.17)

\displaystyle\leq 4^{2p}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big[\mathcal{I}_{0}(t)^{p}+\sigma^{4p}\mathcal{I}_{F;\rm lin}(t)^{p}+\mathcal{I}_{F;\rm nl}(t)^{p}+\sigma^{2p}\mathcal{I}_{B}(t)^{p}\Big].

Lemma 7.3
.

For any
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
, any
T>0
, and any
p\geq 1
, we have the pathwise bounds

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq M^{2p}\|w(0)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p},

(7.18)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\mathcal{E}_{F;\rm lin}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq M^{2p}K_{\rm lin}^{2p}\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)^{p},

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\mathcal{E}_{F;\rm nl}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{p}M^{2p}K_{\rm nl}^{2p}\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)^{p}.

Proof.

These results follow directly from straightforward norm estimates; see also
[
29
, Lem. 5.3]
.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

There exist a constant
K>0
so that for any
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
, any
T>0
, and any
p\geq 1
, we have the pathwise bounds

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{I}_{0}(t)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq K^{2p}\|w(0)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p},

(7.19)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{I}_{F;\rm lin}(t)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq K^{2p}\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)^{p},

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{I}_{F;\rm nl}(t)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{p}K^{2p}\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)^{p}.

Proof.

The key to establish these estimates is to use the time-dependent inner products
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{s;0;k+1}
, as
defined in (
3.90
), and use the equivalence in (
3.91
).
Indeed, the bound for

\mathcal{I}_{0}
follows
by applying Lemma
3.12
with
\delta=0
. The remaining estimates can be obtained
by following the computations in
[
27
, Lem. 9.8–9.12]
, using the identity
(
3.93
) with
\delta=0
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

For any
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
, any integer
T\geq 2
, and any integer
p\geq 1
, we have the bound

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\mathcal{E}_{B}(t)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}+\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{I}_{B}(t)^{p}\leq 2^{p}K_{\rm gr}^{2p}M^{2p}K_{B}^{2p}[\varepsilon^{-1}+\eta]^{p}(p^{p}+\log(T)^{p}).

(7.20)

Proof.

The moment estimate will follow from Proposition
3.18
,
once we have verified that condition (HB) holds.
To see this, we compute

\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\varepsilon(t-s)}\|B(w(s))\textbf{1}_{s\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)}\|_{HS(L^{2}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\leq 2K_{B}^{2}(\varepsilon^{-1}+\eta),

(7.21)

for any
0\leq t\leq T.

In addition, we note that (
7.5
) implies

\displaystyle\|E_{\rm tw}(1)B(w)\|_{HS(L^{2}_{Q};H^{k})}

\displaystyle\leq K_{B}M(1+\|w\|_{H^{k}}),

(7.22)

which yields the bound

\|E_{\rm tw}(1)B(w(t))\textbf{1}_{t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)}\|_{HS(L^{2}_{Q};H^{k})}^{2}\leq 2M^{2}K_{B}^{2}(1+\eta),

(7.23)

for any
0\leq t\leq T.
In conclusion, condition (HB) is satisfied with
\Theta_{*}^{2}=2M^{2}K_{B}^{2}(\varepsilon^{-1}+\eta)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

Collecting the results in Lemmas
7.3
–
7.5
, the estimates (
7.12
)
and (
7.13
) can be combined to yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)^{p}\right]

(7.24)

\displaystyle\leq K^{p}\Bigg(\|v(0)\|_{H^{k}}^{2p}+\sigma^{2p}(p^{p}+\log(T)^{p})+(\sigma^{4}+\eta)^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)^{p}\right]\Bigg).

The result hence readily follows by
restricting the size of
\sigma^{4}+{\eta}
, .
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.5
.

The bound can immediately be deduced from Proposition

7.1
by following the proof of

[
27
, Thm. 2.4]
to undo the time transformation, which only affects the value of the constants. In more detail,
recall the perturbation
v(t)
defined in (
6.1
)
together with the stochastic time transformed perturbation
\bar{v}(\tau)
defined in (
6.18
).
We now write

\bar{N}_{\varepsilon,k}(\tau)=\|\bar{v}(\tau)\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-\varepsilon(\tau-\sigma)}\|\bar{v}(\sigma)\|_{H^{k+1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma,

(7.25)

define the associated stopping time
\bar{t}_{\rm st}(\eta;k),

and recall the similar expressions
(
2.53
) and (
2.54
) for
v(t)
.
Observe the inequality

\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{\rm st}(\eta;k)\wedge T}N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)\leq K_{\kappa}\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq\bar{t}_{\rm st}(K_{\kappa}^{-1}\eta;k)\wedge K_{\kappa}T}\bar{N}_{K_{\kappa}^{-1}\varepsilon;k}(\tau),

(7.26)

where the constant
K_{\kappa}
is as in (
6.17
). This inequality can be obtained by tracing through the proof of
[
27
, Prop. 6.4]
and utilising the implication

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}N_{\varepsilon;k}(t)>\eta\implies\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq K_{\kappa}T}\bar{N}_{K_{\kappa}^{-1}\varepsilon;k}(\tau)>K_{\kappa}^{-1}\eta,

(7.27)

which is equivalent to

t_{\rm st}(\eta)<T\implies\bar{t}_{\rm st}(K_{\kappa}^{-1}\eta)<K_{\kappa}T.

(7.28)

This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
List of main functions

In this appendix we provide an overview of the main functions that are used in this paper.
Throughout this section, we take

k=0
if (Hf-Lip) is satisfied or
k=1

if (Hf-Cub) is satisfied. We assume furthermore
that (HSt), (Hq), and (HCor) hold with this choice of
k
.
In addition, we take
c,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and
\xi\in L^{2}_{Q}
. We emphasise that we have kept our naming conventions as close as possible to those considered in
[
27
,
30
]
to prevent confusion.

We start by
choosing a smooth non-decreasing cut-off function

\chi_{\rm low}:\mathbb{R}\to[\tfrac{1}{4},\infty)

(A.1)

that satisfies the properties

\chi_{\rm low}(\vartheta)=\frac{1}{4}|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1},\quad\vartheta\leq\frac{1}{4}|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1},\quad\chi_{\rm low}(\vartheta)=\vartheta,\quad\vartheta\geq\frac{1}{2}|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1},

(A.2)

together with a smooth non-increasing cut-off function

\chi_{\rm high}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to[0,1]

(A.3)

that satisfies the properties

\chi_{\rm high}(\vartheta)=1,\quad\vartheta\leq 2+\|\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{\rm ref}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\chi_{\rm high}(\vartheta)=0,\quad\vartheta\geq 3+\|\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{\rm ref}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(A.4)

For any
u\in\mathcal{U}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
,
these cut-offs can be used to define

\chi_{h}(u,\gamma)=\chi_{\rm high}(\|u-T_{\gamma}\Phi_{\rm ref}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})})\quad\text{and}\quad\chi_{l}(u,\gamma)=\big[\chi_{\rm low}\big(-\langle u,T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big)\big]^{-1}.

(A.5)

We note that when (HPar) is satisfied and we take

u=T_{\gamma}[\Phi+v]
with

\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\min\{1,|\mathbb{T}|^{\frac{d-1}{2}}[4\|\psi_{\rm tw}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}]^{-1}\},

(A.6)

then we have

\chi_{h}(u,\gamma)=1\quad\text{and}\quad\chi_{l}(u,\gamma)=-\big[\langle u,T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big]^{-1}.

(A.7)

Indeed, we may compute

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|u-T_{\gamma}\Phi_{\rm ref}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}&\leq&\|\Phi-\Phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{\rm ref}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\[5.69046pt]
&\leq&2+\|\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{\rm ref}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})},\end{array}

(A.8)

together with

\begin{array}[]{lcl}|\langle\Phi_{0}-\Phi-v,\psi_{\rm tw}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}|&\leq&\big[\|\Phi_{0}-\Phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big]|\mathbb{T}|^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\|\psi^{\prime}_{\rm tw}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\[5.69046pt]
&\leq&\frac{1}{2}|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1},\end{array}

(A.9)

to conclude

\begin{array}[]{lcl}-\langle\Phi+v,\psi_{\rm tw}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}&=&-\langle\Phi_{0},\psi_{\rm tw}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\langle\Phi_{0}-\Phi-v,\psi_{\rm tw}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\[5.69046pt]
&\geq&|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}-\frac{1}{2}|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}.\end{array}

(A.10)

Again taking
u\in\mathcal{U}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
, we introduce the scalar function

b(u,\gamma)[\xi]=-\chi_{h}(u,\gamma)^{2}\chi_{l}(u,\gamma)\langle g(u)[\xi],T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(A.11)

together with

\kappa_{\sigma}(u,\gamma)=1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|b(u,\gamma)\|^{2}_{HS(L^{2}_{Q};\mathbb{R})},

(A.12)

and the associated quantities

\displaystyle\nu_{\sigma}^{(1)}(u,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\sigma}(u,\gamma)-1,

(A.13)

\displaystyle\nu_{\sigma}^{(-1)}(u,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\sigma}(u,\gamma)^{-1}-1,

(A.14)

\displaystyle\nu_{\sigma}^{(-1/2)}(u,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\sigma}(u,\gamma)^{-1/2}-1.

(A.15)

These expressions are all well-defined by
Corollary
4.9

and Lemma
4.10
.

For
u\in\mathcal{U}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
,
the map
g(u):L^{2}_{Q}\to L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
has a formal adjoint

g^{\rm adj}(u):L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}_{Q},

that acts as

g^{\rm adj}(u)[\zeta]=Qg(u)^{\top}[\zeta],

(A.16)

where the matrix transpose is taken in a pointwise fashion. Indeed,
for
\xi\in L^{2}_{Q}
and
\zeta\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
we compute

\langle g(u)[\xi],\zeta\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle Q^{-1/2}\xi,Q^{-1/2}g^{\rm adj}(u)[\zeta]\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})}=\langle\xi,g^{\rm adj}(u)[\zeta]\rangle_{L^{2}_{Q}}.

(A.17)

The fact that both maps
g(u)
and
g^{\rm adj}(u)

are well-defined follows from Lemmas
4.5

and

4.6
, together with
Lemma
4.4
and
the computation

\begin{array}[]{lcl}||Qg(u)^{\top}[\zeta]||_{L^{2}_{Q}}&=&||Q^{1/2}g(u)^{\top}[\zeta]||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})}\\[5.69046pt]
&\leq&||p||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})}||g(u)^{\top}[\zeta]||_{L^{1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m})}\\[5.69046pt]
&\leq&||p||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})}||g(u)^{\top}||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{m\times n})}||\zeta||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}.\end{array}

(A.18)

This allows us to introduce the
L^{2}_{Q}
-valued function

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{C}(u,\gamma)=\chi_{l}(u,\gamma)\chi_{h}(u,\gamma)Qg(u)^{\top}T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw},

(A.19)

together with the
L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
-valued function

\mathcal{K}_{C}(u,\gamma)=-\chi_{h}(u,\gamma)g(u)\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{C}(u,\gamma)=-\chi_{h}(u,\gamma)^{2}\chi_{l}(u,\gamma)g(u)[g^{\rm adj}(u)T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw}].

(A.20)

Note that a short computation shows

\|b(u,\gamma)\|_{HS(L^{2}_{Q};\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\chi_{h}(u,\gamma)^{4}\chi_{l}(u,\gamma)^{2}\langle g(u)g^{\rm adj}(u)T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw},T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(A.21)

which provides a more explicit representation for (
A.12
).

Now taking
12
12

12

If (Hf-Lip) holds, it suffices to take
u\in\mathcal{U}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
. However (
A.24
) is not necessarily well-defined in this case.

u\in\mathcal{U}_{H^{k+1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
, we are ready to define the scalar function

\begin{array}[]{lcl}a_{\sigma}(u,\gamma;c)&=&-\chi_{l}(u,\gamma)\Big[\langle f(u)+\sigma^{2}h(u),T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}-\langle cu+\sigma^{2}\mathcal{K}_{C}(u,\gamma),\partial_{x}\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\[5.69046pt]
&&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\kappa_{\sigma}(u,\gamma)\langle u,T_{\gamma}\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\Big];\end{array}

(A.22)

see Lemmas
4.11
and
4.24
.
In addition, we define
the
H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
-valued function

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(u,\gamma;c)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\sigma}(u,\gamma)^{-1}\left(f(u)+c\partial_{x}u+\sigma^{2}h(u)+\sigma^{2}[\partial_{x}\mathcal{K}_{C}(u,\gamma)]\right),

(A.23)

where the well-posedness follows from
the bounds (
4.62
)
and (
4.75
),
together with Lemma

4.6

and
Corollary
4.29
.
Note that

\begin{array}[]{lcl}a_{\sigma}(u,\gamma;c)&=&-\chi_{l}(u,\gamma)\kappa_{\sigma}(u,\gamma)\big(\langle\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(u,\gamma;c),T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\langle u,T_{\gamma}\psi_{\rm tw}^{\prime\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big).\end{array}

(A.24)

Exploiting the translational invariance of our nonlinearities and the noise,
we obtain the commutation relations

T_{\gamma}f(u)=f(T_{\gamma}u),\qquad T_{\gamma}g(u)[\xi]=g(T_{\gamma}u)[T_{\gamma}\xi],\qquad T_{\gamma}g^{\rm adj}(u)[\zeta]=g^{\rm adj}(T_{\gamma}u)[T_{\gamma}\zeta].

(A.25)

In particular, we see that

a_{\sigma}(u,\gamma;c)=a_{\sigma}(T_{-\gamma}u,0;c),\qquad b(u,\gamma)[\xi]=b(T_{-\gamma}u,0)[T_{-\gamma}\xi],

(A.26)

and similar identities hold for
\kappa_{\sigma}
,
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}
and the cut-off functions
(
A.5
).
This subsequently allows us to eliminate the dependence on
\gamma
in the sequel.

Assuming (HPar) and taking
v\in H^{k+1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
we introduce the expressions

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}(v;c,\Phi)&=&\Delta_{y}v+\kappa_{\sigma}(\Phi+v,0)\big[\partial_{x}^{2}v+\Phi^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(\Phi+v,0;c)\big]+a_{\sigma}(\Phi+v,0,c)\partial_{x}(\Phi+v),\\[5.69046pt]
\mathcal{S}(v;\Phi)[\xi]&=&g(\Phi+v)[\xi]+\partial_{x}(\Phi+v)b(\Phi+v,0)[\xi].\end{array}

(A.27)

We remark that
\mathcal{S}(v;\Phi)[\xi]\in H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})

and
\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}(v;c,\Phi)\in H^{k-1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
and refer to §
5

for the subtle interpretation of the latter.
By construction, we have
\langle\mathcal{S}(v;\Phi)[\xi],\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0
whenever (
A.6
)
is satisfied.

Finally, recalling the family
(\Phi_{\sigma},c_{\sigma})
constructed in Proposition
2.3
,
we define the
H^{k}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
-valued expressions

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(v)&=&\Phi_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v,0;c_{\sigma})-c_{0}\partial_{x}v-Df(\Phi_{0})v\\[5.69046pt]
&&\qquad\qquad+\kappa_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v;0)^{-1}a_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v,0,c_{\sigma})\partial_{x}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v),\\[5.69046pt]
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(v)[\xi]&=&\kappa_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v,0)^{-1/2}\mathcal{S}(v;\Phi_{\sigma})[\xi],\end{array}

(A.28)

for any
v\in H^{k+1}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Indeed, note that these expressions no longer involve second derivatives of
v
.
In fact, upon introducing the intermediate
function

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{N}_{I;\sigma}(v)&=&\Phi_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v,0;c_{\sigma})-c_{0}\partial_{x}v-Df(\Phi_{0})v\\[5.69046pt]
&=&\Phi_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v,0;c_{\sigma})-[\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}-\partial_{xx}]v,\end{array}

(A.29)

we may use the identity
\mathcal{L}_{\rm tw}^{\rm adj}\psi_{\rm tw}=0
to arrive at the convenient representation

\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(v)=\mathcal{N}_{I;\sigma}(v)-\chi_{l}(\Phi_{\sigma}+v,0)\langle\mathcal{N}_{I;\sigma}(v),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}[\partial_{x}\Phi+\partial_{x}v].

(A.30)

One now readily verifies that
\langle\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}(v),\psi_{\rm tw}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0

holds whenever (
A.6
) is satisfied.

Appendix B
Moment bounds and tail probabilities

We briefly review here the technique of passing back and forth between moment estimates and tail probabilities. Similar results can be found in
[
29
, Sec. 2]
; see also
[
91
,
99
]
.

Lemma B.1
.

Consider a nonnegative random variable
X
. Suppose that there exists two constants
\Theta_{1}>0
and
\Theta_{2}>0
so that the moment bound

\mathbb{E}\left[X^{p}\right]\leq p^{p}\Theta_{1}^{p}+\Theta_{2}^{p}

(B.1)

holds for all integers
p\geq 1
. Then for every
\vartheta>0
we have the estimate

\mathbb{P}(X\geq\vartheta)\leq 3\exp\left(\frac{\Theta_{2}}{2e\Theta_{1}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\vartheta}{2e\Theta_{1}}\right).

(B.2)

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
[
29
, Lem 2.2]
. Let
\lambda>0
be arbitrary. Then by an exponential Markov inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X\geq\vartheta)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\lambda\vartheta}\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda X}]

(B.3)

\displaystyle=e^{-\lambda\vartheta}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{p}}{p!}\mathbb{E}[X^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\lambda\vartheta}\left[\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{p}e^{p}\Theta_{1}^{p}+\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{p}}{p!}\Theta_{2}^{2p}\right],

exploiting the identity
p!\geq p^{p}e^{-p}
in the second inequality above.
Upon choosing
\lambda=(2e\Theta_{1})^{-1}
, we obtain the tail probability
\mathbb{P}(X\geq\vartheta)\leq e^{-\lambda\vartheta}[2+e^{\lambda\Theta_{2}}]\leq 3e^{\lambda\Theta_{2}}e^{-\lambda\vartheta}
, which proves the assertion.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Fix two constants
A\geq 2
and
\Theta_{1}>0
and consider a nonnegative random variable
X
that satisfies the estimate

\mathbb{P}(X\geq\vartheta)\leq 2A\exp\left(-\frac{\vartheta}{2e\Theta_{1}}\right)

(B.4)

for all
\vartheta>0
. Then for any
p\geq 1
we have the moment bound

\mathbb{E}\left[X^{p}\right]\leq\big(p^{p}+\log(A)^{p}\big)(8e\Theta_{1})^{p}.

(B.5)

Proof.

This follows directly from
[
29
, Lem. 2.3]
.
∎

Corollary B.3
.

Consider
N\geq 2
nonnegative random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{N}
and suppose that there exists two constants
\Theta_{1}>0
and
\Theta_{2}>0
so that the moment bound

\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{p}]\leq p^{p}\Theta_{1}^{p}+\Theta_{2}^{p}

(B.6)

holds for all integers
p\geq 1
and each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
. Then for any
p\geq 1
we have the maximal bound

\mathbb{E}\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}X_{i}^{p}\leq\big(p^{p}+\log(N)^{p}+\Theta_{2}^{p}\Theta_{1}^{-p}\big)\left(24e\Theta_{1}\right)^{p}.

(B.7)

Proof.

For any
\vartheta>0
, we invoke Lemma
B.1
and the observation
(2e)^{-1}<1
to obtain the estimate

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}X_{i}\geq\vartheta\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}(X_{i}\geq\vartheta)\leq 3N\exp(\Theta_{2}\Theta_{1}^{-1})\exp\left(-\frac{\vartheta}{2e\Theta_{1}}\right).

(B.8)

The assertion follows by appealing to Lemma
B.2
. In particular, we take
A=\frac{3}{2}N\exp(\Theta_{2}\Theta_{1}^{-1})
, use the inequality
(a+b+c)^{p}\leq 3^{p-1}(a^{b}+b^{p}+c^{p})
and note that
\log(\frac{3}{2})<1
.
∎

Appendix C
Fourier analysis

The Fourier transform can be defined for any locally compact Abelian group
[
6
,
12
,
18
,
78
,
83
]
, and thus in particular for
S\in\mathcal{\{}\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{D}\}
with
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
.
The Pontryagin dual of
S
, denoted by
\widehat{S}
, for the spaces
S=\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
S=\mathcal{D}
are
\widehat{S}=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\widehat{S}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d-1},
respectively.

Let
V
be a seperable Banach space.
For any function
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d};V)\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d};V)
,
we define the Fourier transform to be

\hat{u}(\xi)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(y)e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}\langle\xi,y\rangle}\mathrm{d}y,\quad\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

(C.1)

while for any function
u\in L^{1}(\mathcal{D};V)\cap L^{2}(\mathcal{D};V)
, we have as Fourier transform

\hat{u}(\omega,\xi)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}u(x,y)e^{-2\pi i\omega x}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}\langle\xi,y\rangle}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad(\omega,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}.

(C.2)

The mapping
\mathcal{F}:f\to\hat{f}
extends to an isometric isomorphism from
L^{2}(S;V)
to
L^{2}(\widehat{S};V)
for any choice of
S
.
In particular, the inversion formula for
S=\mathcal{D}
is given by
[
45
,
72
]

u(x,y)=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{u}(\omega,\xi)e^{2\pi i\omega x}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{|\mathbb{T}|}\langle\xi,y\rangle}\mathrm{d}x

(C.3)

and Plancherel’s identity holds, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D};V)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}}\|f(\mathbf{x})\|_{V}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d-1}}\|f(x,y)\|_{V}^{2}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

(C.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|\hat{f}(\omega,\xi)\|_{V}^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\int_{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}\|\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\|_{V}^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}};V)}.

Here we have introduced the notation
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}=\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}\xi,
where
\mathrm{d}\xi
is the counting measure on
\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}.

The factor
1/{|\mathbb{T}|^{d-1}}
is a consequence of not having normalised the Lebesgue induced measure on
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}
.

Recall that the Sobolev spaces
H^{k}(S;V)
can be characterised by means of the Fourier transform
[
68
]
. Indeed, an equivalent norm is given by

\vvvert u\vvvert_{H^{k}(S;V)}=\int_{\widehat{S}}(1+|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2})^{k}\|\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\|_{V}^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi},

(C.5)

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\widehat{S}
.
This equivalence follows readily from the fact that
\widehat{\partial^{\alpha}f}=\xi^{\alpha}\hat{f}
holds and by exploiting Plancherel’s identity. In a similar fashion, Parseval’s identity holds, which yields an inner product on
H^{k}(S;V)
in terms of the Fourier transform. The norm also gives the inner product by polarisation.
